EXTRAORDINARY EMPLOYMENT. Working in a Hot Stove.

Strange occupations, and working under uncommon conditii‘ns are many, but perhaps one orthe queerest is that followed by !Jr. S. Rowell, whose duties compel him to work in a hot stove. Mr. Rowen, who resides at 67, Wellington-street, Keighley, Yorks., gives t full and interesting account of a marvellous cure by Dr. Cassell 's Tablets, of acute inflamation of the kidneys, compltcated by fits, brought on by catching cold whilst engaged in his weird duties. Mr. Rowan writes :—" For the last twelve years I suffered a martyrdom from iuflammation of ' the kidneys and fits, which was brought on by catching cold when working in a hot stove. I could neither sleep nor eat, and wasted away to a mere skeleton, I could only walk with difficulty. I tried every possible remedy, and was under seven doctors, who all told me my case was- a mystery. I was in this helpless condition when I began to take Dr. Cassell's Tablets. I found 1 myself improved after the first bottle, and now I after taking four I feel strong and well, and am able to walk twenty miles a day without any fatigue." Surely this striking evidence will convince all sufferers of the extraordinary curative power of Dr. Casselrs Tablets. They stand supreme for all forms oi nerve or bodily weakness, or such , complaints as indigestion, weakness of the kidneys and back, palpitation, loss of flesh or appetite, weak lungs, wrecked constitution and nervcus prostration. They possess the peculiar virture of converting fat into sound healthy flesh, blood, bone, and muscle. The price is on'y 10111., and any chemist can supply them.

JUNIOR LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

• Annual Meeting. The Warrington Junior Liberal Association inaugurated what promises to be a successful session on MondaY evening when the annual business meeting was held et Gregory's Cafe, Buttermarket-street. Mr. R. E. Burtt presided over a fair attendance which included :-- Messrs. Harold Gray and H. W. Guest (hon. secretaries) and Mr. J. Derbyshire (hon. treasurer). LAST SESSION'S WORK. Reporting upon last session's work, Mr. GUEST said the most successful evenings were those devoted to Socialism. He urged that more interest should be taken in the historical and biographical sections,because he believed if political history were studied more there would be less eagerness to run after Socialism. ElistorY showed that the best work had been done by individuals. On the whole they had a very good session. Some extremely interesting papers were read and he was sere the members derived a great deal of benefit from there. The greatest stride they took was in amalgamating it the Young British Liberals' Federation as that ha' brought them into touch with a large organization which would eventually prove of great service to there. The Association could also congratulate itself upon the success of the public meeting in the Parr Hall wile° Mr. Sydney Buxton, M.P., was the principal speaker. The report was adopted. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. RESIGNATION OF MR. GRAY. _

When the election of officers came up for consider tion, Mr. GUEST said he sincerely regretted to infore! the members that owing to business taking him out ot town, Mr. Gray was unable to continue the joint sere- taryshir. There could be no greater Icss to the Associ- ation than his resignation, for it was largely, if o 0! entirely due to his efforts that the organisation eel' into existence and his services had been invaluable. Mr. Guest niored that the resignation be accepted with regret and that a vote of thanks be accorded to Air' Gray _for his work.

TESTED IN WARRINGTON.

A very important matter has been put to the test Warrington, and here is the wonderful result. Mr. Joseph Price lives just near the Market Plecec; Warrington, at 46, Queen-street, and it is him we bale to thank for the following statement. "Four years egi I had an awful attack of lumbago," he said to one our representatives when telling him the facts of 0;,1 case. '1 got up one morning in terrible pain—l OO; scarcely get out of bed and dress myself, and the efferj to get to work mused me to perspire, such agony vA° in. The work I was•on was not heavy, but it took half an hour to do what I should have done in Pi. minutes ; the torture was unbearable. The overSe.e, asked me what was wrong with me, and when I told be said I was not to work in that state. Then I set 07 for home, but how I reached there I do not know, a° the perspiration fairly rolled off me. " My arms wore all right, and the shoulders, too bt I had no use in the lower part of my body, m/legt being powerless. The urinary secretions were unnattio and scalded. I got about with two sticks, and ti neighbour seeing me and asking what was the tnatter; said 'lf you do as I tell you, you'll get off those sticit,s in a very short time ' ; her advice was to try Des°, pills, so my son went for a box. I had not taken till; doses of these before I could do without the stics..; and within a week I started work again. Since the'r I've never been without Doan's pills, and I've e,e/,,,et had an' trouble with my back. WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE. THE FEEDING IF SCHOOL C;IILDREN.

Another Animated Discussion.

THE FUTURE OF THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Eincation Committee was held at the Town Hall on Monday evening under tha presidency of Conncillor J. Fultou Smith. There were also present : —Tan Mayor (Alder- man Smethurst), Aldermen Benne!,t,Tinnioo. Bolton,and Evans, Councillors Ellison, Barton, Lord, Wilkinson, Pemberton, Ball, Toole, Nisld, Dicensri. and Clegg, Revs. Canon Morley Stevenson, J. fonge and N. Wilson, Miss Williams, Nlessrs. H. Roberts (deputy chairman). C. H. Unsworth. A. J. Jolley, E. Plinston, T. P. Bradshaw, R. T. Fa.irolough, G. H. Kingdon, G. Walker, J. Moore Nlnrrry (secretary and director) and J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer). CARETAKERS AND THE BATI3.

At a meeting of the Finance Committee a letter was read from the Secretary of the Warrington and District Caretakers' Federation, asking the Committee to take steps with a view to securing the admission of the care- takers of the schools in the Borough to the public baths at a reduced charge, hut the Committee could not see their way to take any action themselvel in the matter, but referred the Federation to the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee. The minutes were approved. GRATIFYING EXAMINATION RESULTS. In moving the adoption of the minutes of the aphool Management Committee, Canon STEVENSON referred to the summary of the results of the examina- tions in scieace, art, and commercial subjects for the session 1906-7. He said that on the whole the com- mittee might congratulate themselves upon a steady if not a very great. improvement. With regard to the _City and Guilds of London Institute the apparent fall- ing off in the number of those who entered for the examinations was only apparent, as some of the sub- jects such as brickwork, masonry, and mechanical engineering appeared under other heads. One of the most satisfactory parts of the committee's, work was the list of successes under the auspices of the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes. There was a steady increase in every respect for the good, and four distinctions had been attained, which was very honour- able indeed for a town of the size of Warrington. In the branch technical schools there was a large advance in the number of passes in the intermediate branches of study and that was a satisfactory feature. THE FUTURE OF THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
- - - - The School Management Committee considered their recent interview with the governors of the Warrington Grammar school with reference to the new regulations of the Board of Education for Secondary schools, and, after some discussion, it was resolved, that' in order to enable the governors of the Grammer school to have further time in which to consider their position as affected by the new regulations, the Board of Education be informed, in accordance with Article 43 of the regulations, that the school is, in the view of the Com- mittee, required as part of the Secondary school provision for their area, and that, for a period of one year, the conditions set forth in Articles 5, 18 kb), 23, and 24 may be waived. Canon STEVENSON said it would be within the recol- lection of the committee that not very long ago at very considerable expense of time and thought a new scheme was drawn up for the Grammar school and he thought it gave satisfaction both to the governors of the Grammer school and to the Education Committee. It provided for a considerable increase in representatives from the Town Council on the go ',ern- ing body—an increase which could not come into effect immediately without the removal of some of the exist- ing members but which as years went on would come into effect until the representation of the town upon the governing body would be very considerable. Hardly had that scheme begun to get into working order when new regulations were issued by the Board of Education affecting all secondary • schools and • among others, of• course, the Grammar School. In order to get the highest possible grant the Governors would have to adopt certain reeulations which would , materially alter the scheme to which they had agreed. The new regulations were issued at an extremely incon- venient time—just before the summer holidays—and it was impossible during the summer months for all the governors to meet together. Then came the lamented death of the Headmaster, so that with one thing and another it was impossible for the governors to meet and give consideration to the new conditions. As soon as they were able to do so they approached the School Management Committee and asked them to consider whether they would not take advantage of Clause 43 of the regulations which permitted the Education I Authority to certify that any particular schonl was ' necessary Mr the secondary education of the district and that if the authority certified that, the schaal. was allowed to receive the highest grant without coming under the regulations. It . seemed to the School Management Committee that that was an eminently reasonable thing. The governors of the school would have( a year in which to consider the position and .the highest grant would be continued. He had no doubt that before the expiration of the year a satisfactory agreement would be arrived at. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19071026_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

Canon STEVEgSON announced that the Dean of Man- chester, who was at one time headmaster at Harrow, had kindly promised to distribute the prizes to the students of the School of Art, the Secondary School and TechnicAl lustituteand the branch Technical Schools, in the Parr Mil on November 26th, and it was earnestly hoped that there would be a large representa- tion of the committee on that occasion. LEARNT TO SiVI-M.

A summary of the returns which had been received from the head teachers of the public elementary schools with reference to the attendance of boys at the baths during the season 1907 was submitted, from which it appealed that, in all, SS boys had attended the baths during the season, and that, as a result, 418 boys had learnt to mina, 20 being already able to do, so. Canon STEVENSONI said that although he was not quite sure that the figures were absolutely accurate—it was very difficult to say exactly what was meant by being able to swim—at all events, they were so substantially accurate as to mean that some hundreds of boys had in the past season been able to become more or less swimmers. That was something upon which the Committee could congratulate themselves. The only thing one could not help deploring was that the accommodation was so insufficient that it was impossible to provide for the 6irls. He hoped that in future when new schools were built accommodation would he pro- vided so that every scholar in the borough would have the opporinnity of len-fling that very healthy and useful exercise.

FAIRFIELD SCHOOL. AMALGANIATION PROPOSALS.

A letter having been submitted to the School Manage- ment Committee from the managers of the Fairfield school in reference to the proposed amalgamation of the two girls' departments of that school, it was resolved that the managers of the Fairfield school be informed that this Committee, having carefully considered the views of the managers upon the matter, are of opinion that it is desirable that the two girls departments should be amalgamated, and that it is proposed to apply to the Board of Education for their sanction to the arrange- ment. At a subsequent meeting at the request of the managers of the Fairfield school, it was decided to receive a deputation from the managers in reference to the proposed amalgamation of the two girls' depart- ments, at the next meeting of the Committee. The whole of the School Management Committee's minutes were approved. NIEALS FOR SCHOOL CHILDREN. INTERESTING REPORT AND ANINIATED DISCUSSION.

In accordance with an undertaking given at the last meeting the minutes of the School Attendance Com- mittee contained a report prepared by the Director in connection with the Education (Provision of Meals), Act, 1905. It stated :—The information has in the first instance, been obtained from the Head Teachers, and then, as far as possible, verified by the Officers of this Committee. On the 26th February the Head Teachers wore asked to give the number of children attending their schools who were insufficiently fed either through carelessness or through neglect : 277 children were reported us insufficiently fed, 191 being so on account of poverty, and 86 on account of neglect. The return showed that, of the 191, no less than 75 were in attendance at the Latchford St. .lames' and Latch- ford R.C. schools. 32 were in ...attendance at the St. Benedict's nod 16 at the St. Mary's schools, so that no less than 123 out of the 191 were in attendance at four schools, leaving 68 of the other cases distributed in the remaining schools of the town. With reference to the children suffering from neglect, there- Nero 11 at the Latchford R.C., 18 at the St. Barnahas', 15 at the St. Peter's, and 12 at the Wycliffe school, or 56 out of the 81 neglected children were in attendance at font schools. These returns show • that the insufficiently fed or neglected children nearly all attend the same schools and persnmahly come from the same districts. It would be foolish to blame the heed teachers for not being competent relieving officers.He submitted that to ask the head teachers to take on, in addition to their other multifarious duties, the duties of relieving officers was asking too much of them and expecting them to do a class of work which required a great deal of experience and knowledge of human nature—experi- ence. which head teachers were not likely, in the ordinary course of their avocation, to acquire. He was not blaming the teachers but the figures surely showed that head teachers of the schools which really repre- sented the poorest section of the population had got different results from certain other teachers and he therefore suggested that the figures were unreliable and untrustworthy, and that being so, did not justify the Committee's decision. Alderman Bolton and he were severely castigated at the last meeting for their temerity in daring to dissent from the proposal of the School Attendance Committee. The suggestion was made by more than one speaker that anyone who dared to dissent was actuated by improper feelings with regard to the children and the welfare of the town. (Mr. H. Roberts: "No.") He for one strongly resented any suggestion of that kind. He submitted that these questions ought to be approached in a judicial and scientific spirit, and sentimental twaddle ought not to he substituted for argument. At the last meeting, they were exposed to a tremendous cannonade from Councillor Wilkinson. (Laughter.) After the smoke and noise had cleared away no one seemed very much the worse for it. With all due res- pect to Councillor Wilkinson, his big guns were not weapons of precision, and his arguments very often went off the wrong way and knocked his own feat from under him. (Laughter.) Afterwards they were sub- jected to a more dangerous, though less noisy fire from his good friend the Deputy-Chairman, who did use weapons of precision, but on that occasion if he had given them a little more argument and a little less rhetoric it would have been distinctly advantageous to them all. (Laughter.) What he (Mr.. Jolley) said at the last meeting was not said lightly or hastily. He firmly adhered to the position he took up, and he was strongly of the opinion that if the suggestion he adopted, it was simply taking another step towards sapping and undermining parental responsibility, wnich was too serious a thing to be done lightly, and in effect it was offering a premium out of the rates on alcoholic and sexual intemperance. Alderman BOLTON seconded the amendment. Councillor WILKINSON said he was not in the habit of firing off any big guns, that he was aware of. He always endeavoured to speak as his mind and conscience prompted him. He claimed to have had the experience of a life time in regard to the poorer classes with which the Act would deal. He wanted the teacher immediately he found that a child was badly fed, to be able to report it to the proper authority. At present they had no authority except the degrading Union— which he hoped to see abolished before he died. Men and women worked hard and brought a family up and that was all the town had to offer them. He hoped there would he some new legislation, such as the grant- ; ing of old age pensions. It was one of the easiest I things to accomplish if obstacles yvere not placed in the way. When the Town Council had adopted the Act I they could consider whether it was necessary to put it - 1 into operation. Then would be , the time for Mr. Jolley to object. Mr. R. T. FAIRCLOUGH : Keep your gun powder dry. (Laughter.) Councillor WILKINSON: Oh yes. I keep my powder dry. (Laughter.) Continuing he said he thought Mr. Jolley, with his large experience of Poor Law work, would have been of the first to agree with the adoption of the Act. The committee were not going to give carte blanche for the supply of free breakfasts to children from off the streets. or undermine parental control. They were going to find the children whose parents neglected them through bad trade or sickness, and help them. In cases where parents tried to evade their responsibility they could be made to refund the money expended. Councillor PEMBERTON said be was rather surprised at Mr: Jolley's attitude, because he had always thought he was a progressive. If it was in the interests of the State to educate the child, surely it was in the interests of the State to see that the child grew up strong, healthy, and fit to do a good day's work. It did not matter to the committee why a child was not being properly' fed—at any rate that would not justify them I- in withholding food. Their first duty was to feed the children with the food they needed. STOCKTON HEATH. child and then see where the neglect came in. ANOTHER SCHOOL SUGGESTED.

At a meeting of the Site and Buildings Committee the Special Sub-Committee reported th# proceedings with respect to the provision of sites for schools, and, after some discussion, it was resolved that the surveyor be requested to obtain and submit to the committee a plan showing the amount of land required for a public elementary school.

The sketch plans of the proposed alterations to the Secondary School and Technical Institute, which had been prepared by the Surveyor, were considered, and it was resolved, that the seine be approved by this com- mittee, and be submitted to the Gymnasium Committee for their approval ; and thqt the Surveyor be requested to prepare the necessary plans for submission to the Board of Education.

Aldermen BOLTON, rho moved the adoption of the minutes, proposed an'alterltion of the first resolution, as he maintained that the Surveyor was only instructed toob thin informatiog and not to prepare a plan. The feeling of the members of the committee was that a plan wns intended, and the minutes were approved as submitted.

Charge Against a Schoolboy. ____ _

Thomas:Wooil, aged 12, whose parents reside at 54, Plumpton-street, was charged at the Warrington Occasional Court on Monday with stealing an over- coat, the property of Stanley Ditchfield, aged 12, of Slyne Head Farm, Sankey. The boy Ditchfield said he attended Sankey Council School, and at three o'clock on October 17th he saw his overcoat hanging in the lavatory. When he came out at four o'clock it bad gone and he informed the scho lmaster. Thomas went to the same school, but witness could not say whether he was present on the day in question.— John Lee, assistant to Mr. Briggs, rawnbroker, said the boy Wood came to the shop on the 18th with the overcoat and asked for 2s. 6d. upon it. He said the overcoat belonged to a Mrs. Bowman, and as witness knew her he advanced the money.- On the applica- tion of Sergeant Smith, Wood was remanded to the County Petty Sessions to be held on November 6th. The boy's father went bail in the sum of -£lO.

PADGATE.

THE RAILWAY MISSION.—On Monday evening a meeting in connection with this mission was held in the Padgate C.E. School. the Vicar presiding. The principal speaker was Miss Given, hon. superintendent of the Liverpool Railway Mission, who gave a graphic descrip- tion of the work being carried out by the mission among railway employees. ORGAN-GRINDER RUN DOWN BY MOTOR.

County Court Sequel to Penketh

Collision.

Harry Walmsley, an organ-grinder of Warrington, appeared as plaintiff, before his Honour Judge Thomas, in the Liverpool County Court on Monday, when he claimed damages from Robert Hudson, of Ruskin-street, Liverpool, in respect of damages to his organ and death of a pony. ..•_ _ _ _

Mr. F. Cuthbert Smith (instructed by Mr. Thomas S. Steel, of Warrington) appeared for the plaintiff who, he stated, met with an accident twenty-four years ago, and as a result lost an arm. He was set up in business as an organ-grinder by sympathisers, who organised a subscription on hit behalf, and bad since supported himself by playing the organ in and around the district of Warrington. On the 4th June he was proceeding to Widnes, his organ being drawn by a pony, and when in Farnworth-road, Penketh, he met a motor-engine which was being driven by the defendant. The road at the point under notice is about thirty feet wide, and there was quite sufficient room for the two vehicles to pass each other with ease. The engine just missed the plaintiff's cart, but the furniture van attached to the motor caught the off wheel of the organ, knocked off the cap, and dragged the plaintiff, organ, and pony about a dozen yards. The pony was killed instantaneously, and the organ suffered considerable damage. Defendant it was alleged, did not stop, but continued along the road, the plaintiff running after him for about two miles, when defendant, owing to shortness of water, was obliged to pull up. When plaintiff accused defendant of having broken up the organ and killed the pony, defendant, it was stated, denied that he bad ever seen plaintiff or that he knew anything about him. It was stated by one of plaintiff's witnesses that defendant was driving the motor at the rate of eight to ten miles an hour, and that the vehicle was wobbling all over the road. To repair the damages to the organ was estimated to cost about .£9.

befendant said he could not see that he was liable tecause he never saw the man's pony or anything get touched whatever. He produced a card to show that he was a practical and competent engineer. He had had twenty-three years' experience in road work; he had been in Africa and at sea, and had worked hard all his life, and it was very bard that the plaintiff should come there and say he (defendant) shad done the damage. Plaintiff was better off than him. He (defendant) did not earn more than 21 a week. He added, " I am thinking of chucking this job up and going back to my trade: lam fed up with it." His Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for .£3O and costs, payable in fourteen days.

TELLURIt. Tellurium, states the Engineering and Mining Journal, is an element almost with- out use in the arts; small amounts are used in medicine, but the output of one large copper refinery for one day would, it it said, stock a large chemical supply house with enough tellurium to last a year.

HIGH TEMPERATURES.

High temperatures now play an important part in our daily work, and it is interesting, says Science Siftings, to note the following actual temperatures of familiar objects and operations, as determined by a new optical pyrometer. Porcelain furnace, 2,498 deg. F.; glass furnace, 2,552c1eg. ; open hearth steel, 2,795 deg. ; melted platinum, 3,236 deg. ; in- candescent lamp, 3,272 deg.; arc lamp, 7,410 deg.; the sun, 13,712 deg.

IS THE PEARL A DISEASE?

The irritation which causes the deposition of pearls in oysters appears to be variously produced. In a report on the pearl oyster fisheries of 'Ceylon, Professor M. A. Herd- man states that some pearls appear to be formed round grains of sand, and some of inferior quality are probably disease calculi (stones), but most of the best pearls are de- posited round a parasitic worm. Pearly ex- crescences on the interior of the, shell are due to irritation caused by boring animals.

DANGEROUS AIR IN CLOSED ROOMS. M. Albert Levy, the celebrated French chemist, who has been experimenting with the quality of air in closed rooms, particu- larly with the lack of oxygen produced in dwellings by cooking, heating, lighting, and the breathing of the inmates, has declared that while some of the poisonous gases are easily discernible through the sense of smell, one of the most importpt—the oxide of car- bon—being odourless, is especially dangerous. It is apt to cause sr.‘,vere poisoning without the least "advance notice" of the effect, and this with very small quantities ofi the noxious gas. M. Levy has, therefore, con- structed an apparatus which he claims will indicate a quality as low as 1-100,000 th part of oxide of carbon in the air of a closed room.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAT AND LIGHT RAYS.

The rays of heat and light are quite inde- pendent of each other in their ability to penetrate different substances. For illustra- tion, glass allows the sun's heat to pass through as readily as it does the rays of light, .and that without heating the glass too. If the glass be coated with lampblack, however, the rays of light are arrested, but the heat passes through as before, not a single degree's difference in the latter phe- nomenon being noticeable. 'then, again, both heat and light pass through water, provided it is clear.„ One of the oddities in this con- nection is this: Although the heat and light pass through the water in its normal state, the addition of a little powdered alum, which readily dissolves without leaving the least murkiness, will arrest the rays of beat to such an extent as almost immediately to raise the temperature of the water to a per- ceptible degree.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
KITES AND THE WEATHER.

Of recent .years there has been a great ex- tension of the method of ascertaining records of atmospheric conditions by means of speci- ally designed kites and balloons. Rubber balloons sufficiently light to carry self-record- ing instruments can be sent up to a height of from ten to fifteen miles. The records they have brought back with them have wrought havoc, says the W estniinster Gazette, with pre- vailing. theories of the dynamics of the atmo- sphere. Kites are sent up with winds varying at the surface from ten to even forty miles an hour. When the velocity of the wind is less than ten miles an hour the captive balloon is in request. The instruments carried by the kites and balloons may weigh as much as 41b., and they bring to the workers below records of the humidity, temperature, and pressure of the air, and of the velocity of the wind. In the case of the free balloons, which ASCEND TO GREAT HEIGHTS,

===== 0002601_19071026_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
the thermographs which accompany theta may have to experience great changes of tem- perature. It is, therefore, necessary to test carefully the accuracy of the instruments at low points of temperature. For this purpose the meteorological observatories are equipped with a plant for the manufacture of liquid air. The relation between the solar radiation and terrestrial weather conditions is also a branch of solar physics which is at present at- tracting much attention. Advances have been made, but progress is handicapped by want of a special building and apparatus. This, no doubt, will be remedied in due course. We know that 'changes in the direction and the INTENSITY OF THE MAGNETIC FORCE

as registered at the earth's surface are syn- chronous with the appearance and disappear- ance of well4ecognised phenomena . observed on the face of the sun. The magnetism of the earth is an extremely delicate instrument for recording the variations in the solar activi- ties. Magnptic force ebbs and flows with the annual approach and recession of the sun, with its daily march through the heavens, and its rotation on - its axis. A sun spot is

immediately betrayed by a corresponding dis- turbance of the earth's magnetism. ' Hence the importance of a continuous record of tile magnetic variations at the earth's surface.

ASTRONOMICAL PROBLEMS.

Astronomers regard the hundred million stars that stud the heavens as suns, says the New York Tribune. While they have prac- tically no evidence that those bodies have families of planets like those which go cir- cling around the central orb of our solar system, they concede that the Supposition is at least credible. In size, temperature, and other characteristics the satellites of other suns may differ among themselves, as' do the sister planets of the earth. If, therefore, it could be demonstrated that any of the latter was the abode of creatures endowed with in- lellect it would' not be unreasonable to be- lieve that thousands—perhaps millions— of other worlds were inhabited by

It is particularly fortunate that the planet which in its apparent fitness to sus- tain life most nearly resembles the earth ,can be studied more advantageously than the other members of the - family to which it be- longs. \ ehus at times comes as close to the eal•th as Mars doe's, but it then presents its dark side for inspection. Since the orbit of Mars lies outside the- earth's, the ruddy planet oCers a brilliantly illuminated surface to terrestrial observers when the interval be- tween tll.. two bodies reaches a minimum. Besides. Mars is not enveloped, as most of the other planets seem to be, in a veil of clouds, and the absence of such a screen won- derfully facilitates scrutiny. WOMEN'S CHAT.

LIVERPOOL AND NORTH WALES STEAMSHIP COMPANY.

DIVIDEND FIVE PER CENT.

The following is the report of the directors of the above dompany :—The directors present the report and statement of accounts of the seventeenth, year's working of the company's steamers. Notwithstanding the unfavourable weather experienced during the greater part of the season the steamers have carried a fair number of passengers, and the result, as shown by the profit and loss account, may ho considered as satisfac- tory under the circumstances. The increase shown in working expenses is mainly owing the advance in the price of coal, and to the extension of the company's business. During the preSent year the company pur- chased a small steamer for employment in the coasting branch of their business in North Wales. Th 4 steamer has been named St. Elian, and took up her position in the service in July. The various branches of the com- pany's service have been performed with regularity, and the steamers have been well kept up. After deducting the sum of £5,215 17s. 11d, for depreciation,the amount available for dividend and other purposes, including the balance brought forward frozn.last season, is £4,519 19s. lid:, out of which the directors recommend the payment of a dividend of 5 per cent. per annum, free of income tax, carrying forward a balance of L 1,334 19s. 11d. The retiring directors are Mr: 11. Maclver and Sir R. Williams Bulkeley, Bart., who are elgible for re-election. The retiring auditors, Messrs. Chalmers, Wade, and Co., are also eligible for re-appointment.

RAILWAY COMPANY SUED.

Passenger Injured by Carriage Door.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday. before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., Jacob Whittle, fireman. employed at Collins Green Colliery, and resid- ing in Choral Terrace, Collins Green, sued the London and North-Western Rail Way Company for damages for injuries alleged to have been caused by the negligence of one of the company's servants. Mr. IL J. Longton, of Warrington, appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Lambert represented the railway company—Plaintiff's case was that on June 15th he travelled from Earlestown to C)llins Green by the 10.40 p.m. train. The door of the compartment was left open until the train had started and plaintiff got up to close it. He was pulling the door to when a servant of the company suddenly banged it and plaintiff's left hand was caught between tie door and the post, the second finger being badly crushed. In consequence of the accident plaintiff was unable to work for six weeks and had to meet a doctor's hill for ES sg.—Evidence was given by the plaintiff, Dr. Walker. and several passengers in the compartment. It was admitted that there was no hurry, and that all the passengers were comfortably seated when the Plaintiff voluntarily got up to close the door.—Mr. Lambert submitted that there was absolutely no sugges- tion of a case for him to answer. If there had been hurry and the porter had shut the door before the passengers had time to get in and plaintiff's finger had been crushed, he (Mr. Lambert) would not have bad the slightest chance of defending the action. But there being no hurry it saved him entirely from an unfavour- able verdict. Plaintiff took upon himself the functions of a railway servant and shut the door—a thing which nobody asked him to do, and which there was no necessity for him to do. As a matter of fact the door was closed by the person appointed to close it. It was said that the door was banged when the train was on the move but that was a perfeetly common practice—dangerous for persons who put their hands between the door and the post but otherwise free from anything but sentimental objection. It was incumbent on plaintiff to remain in his seat and hot to Put himself needlessly in the way of danger.—His Honour said plaintiff's action led to the inference that he thought that if the door had remained open there might have been danger to the passagers, and he there- fore tried to close it. It was a reasonable act on the part of plaintiff when he found the train had started and the door was still open, to endeavour to close it. His hand was not Dot on the door post for no purpose and merely through negligence. Tt was put there in the reasonable hope of shutting the door and so preventine damage to himself and the rest of the passengers. He gave judgment for Plaintiff for £25 (the amount having previously been agreed upon between the contesting parties in the event of the company being held liable)—Mr. Lambert asked for stay of execution as he thohght the Company would take the case further—His Honour refused the application, pointing out that under the ordinary rules of the Court the door was open for the Company to pursue the case.

Buckley-street Methodist Free Church. Particular interest centred in the chapel anniversary services held at this church on Sunday as the pulpit was occupied by the "Boy Preacher" of Blackburn, Master Robert Ainsworth, who gave two very effective addresses, preaching in the morning from the parable of the Ten Virgins, and in the evening from the parable of the Prodigal Son. There were very large congrega- •tions. The choir rendered anthems under the conductor- ship of Mr. Nathan Cook, assisted by Miss M.Plinston, the organist of the church. In the afternoon a very enjoyable service was gone through before another splendid congregation, when Master Ainsworth gave an address, illustrated by many a pathetic story. Coun- cillor Steel mo4pied the chair, and asked the congrega- tion to follow the doctrines laid down by Jesus Christ. Miss L. Plinston sang in a delightful manner. " Lead Thou me on " and " Before the shrine," ter rich con- tralto voice being heard to splendid advantage. Mr. J. Corrin . very effectively rendered the two solos "Pilgrims of the night "and "Holy City." Miss M. Plinston was the accompanist. Mr., T. Cook and Mr. H. Woolley also took part in the service. The Royal Visits.—Quite a round of Royal visits begin this week, with the return of Qoeen Alexandra, and the arrival of the King and Queen of Norway. Like the visit of the King and Queen of Spain, who are expected next week, and will bring the little Prince of Asturias with them, that of the King and Queen of I Norway is • a private one, and after a short stay in London, their Majesties will join the family gathering at Sandringham, to celebrate the King's birthday on November 9th. These festivities will precede the State visit of the German Emperor and Empress, who are to arrive at Windsor Castle on November 11th. Their ‘Tajesties will be entertained by the City of London on Wednesday, November 11th, and thAr visit, will last to the end of the week. The King and Queen of Spain will stay for the first part of their visit with Princ,ese Henry of Battenberg at Kensington Palace. Of Princess Henry's three sons—Queen Ena's brothers— Prince Alexander the eldest is a lieutenant in the Navy, Princp Leopold who is the delicate one, isat Cambridge, and Prince Maurice the youngest is at Wellington College. Queen Victoria's Home Life.—The devotion of Queen Victoria to her husband.and children, is the outstanding feature in the three volumes of her confidential corres- pondence edited by Mr. A. C. Benson and Lord Esher, I and published by authority of the King. Most of us remember Queen Victoria as a widowed woman, who, living in the presence of a great sorrow, seldom emerged from the seclusion of her home circle, but" The Letters of Queen Victoria "—issued by Mr. John Murray at £3 3s. net—reveal to us the joyous, high-spirited Queen, happy, beyond measure in her married life, evincing the keenest interest in the doings of Society, and directing State affairs with that practical commonsense and Tisdom, which made her reign such a blessing to 64) nation. The correspondence only deals with the period of her married life 1837-1861, during which, most of her thoughts and time were devoted to the Prince Consort, and her family, but the political contents of these volumes show that Queen Victoria had much more to do in directing State affairs than has been supposed. Queen Victoria was never very partial to men of great literary, or the scientific attainments' at least in early life, but she was an excellent judge of character. Her two great favourites were Lord Melbourne, and the Duke of Wellington. but many leading men whom she disliked at first, such as Sir Robert Peel, Bright,Cobden,Disraeli and Gladstone. ske came afterwards to esteem for their high personal chlracter and public services. Children's Happy Evenings.—The difficulty of know- ing what to do in the winter evenings is a problem that vexes many people. They might, however' take a hint from Lady Jersey's work among poor children, and at once add to their resources for entertaining them- selves, as well as others. In every neighbour there is the opportunity for bringing brightness and pleasure into narrow and dull young lives, by organizing or helping in the organization of children's " happy evenings." The help required is from those who can tell or read a fairy tale, get up children's theatricals, supervise game, romps and dances, and other forms of innocent pleasure. There are always to be found some people with a natural gift for entertaining the young, and in many localities it only wants someone to take the initiative to secure the help of others, either by their services, or some small contribution to the trifling expenses that need be incurred. The Autumnal Cold.—Just now the whole nation appears to be divided in about equal proportions into , two sections, those who are sneezing, and those who are sneezed at. In the old superstitious days, people used to make a sign when they sneezed, and it is seldom indeed, that one can enter a railway carriage or omnibus, without having to invoke a blessing on oneself. Sneezing is generally going on more or less, so that one has but little chance of avoiding falling a victim to the autumnal cold. Sometimes a fellow passenger adopts as a safeguard the sucking of a peppermint or aniseed drop, which adds still further to the aggrava- tion by filling the vehicle with an offensive odour. Sooner or later the problem of how to avoid the autumnal cojd, becomes one too many for us. It seems to defy all attempts to circumvent it, and spares neither rich nor poor. The Long Velvet Skirt.—So great an affection have many women for the short skirt, that they even insist upon velvet skirts, intended for day wear—being cut to clear the ground. This is a huge mistake. Velveteen it is possible to treat in this way but not velvet, which we associate with smart afternooa toilettes, or stately evening gowns. For both purposes the skirt should touch the ground in front, sweep it at the sides, and boast a moderate train. The newest tones in velvets are remarkably good, some especially lovely shades being found in dull blue, purple with a touch of red in it, and brown with a flavor of green in its composition. One Colour Economical.—Undoubtedly the " one-d colour " girl or woman can dress upon a much smallerl sum than her sister who includes many colours in her outfit, moreover she very frequently presents the better appearance. And there need be no monotony about the notion. For example, one year the "one-colour" girl can keep to blue, another year to green, yet another to brown, and so on. , Many changes too can be rung by means of the various shades of the colour, which of course should all tone together. Thus hats, cats, skirts, under-skirts, en-tout-ces, and all accessories, are possible wear one with another, and much expense is spared, An Old-World idea.—Little handbags, made of at, same material as the gown, are carried by many smart women, the handles being of knotted ribbon, and the clasps of steel, silver or gold. Usually a little exqui- site embroidery appears upon the fabric, the colours employed matching those found in the trimmings of the gown. The notion is a pretty, old-world idea, but I we shall do well not to trust our purse or valuables in this dainty little receptacle, as the notion altogether, especially the ribbon handles, is likely to be highly approved by the light-fingered fraternity. Tailor-Made and Frilled Skirts.—There are two innovations in connection with the tailor-made costume, one is the inevitable cut-away threequarter coat, and the other the accompanying frilled skirt front. Provided we can afford to lay our tailor-mades aside at the end of the season, there is nothing to be said against the cut-away fronts but if we have to "make them last," it would be safer to be faithful to the square fronts. The cut-away front is a fashion that will date, and should therefore be severely passed over by habitual hard-ups." With regard to the frilled skirt front, it is well to bear in mind the fact that it does not suit every woman, especially when the collar- band corresponds and a little frill of the lace appears, to outline the top of the neck. Fortunate individuals with long slender swan-like throats, can wear such a neckband and front successfully, but the great majority or women look their best in a plain neckband and front, simply tucked. and inlet with lace or embroidery. The New, Spanish Scarves.—On these' days when furs are likely, to prove too heating, fashionable women are wearing Spanish crepe de chene scarves, u.ith deep fringes. The young Queen of Spain is especially fond of these pretty wraps, and during the past few months has despatched several to young friends in this country. They are now to be had in many of the best shops, and in all the most delicate and beautiful colours. The scarves are passed once round the neck, crossed behind, and then drawn forward again, the fringed ends falling stole fashion, almost to the feet.

wretched Fanny by the arm, and led her out of court. _ _

The Earl had sunk back and covered his face with his hand. The magistrates gazed straight before them, with diirgust and indignation plainly expressed on their faces. Talbot Denby looked round for a moment or two then with a scornful, defiant shrug of the shoulders he walked to the private entrance and disappeared. Some of the people made for the door at the same moment, but a policeman kept them back. There was some noise and confusion, and when the clerk had sternly called for silence the case was resumed.

Burchett was put into the box to prove the finding of the knife; the other evidence for the prosecution was taken, but Mr. Selby seemed to pay little or no attention. He sat with his head resting in his hand, his eyes fixed on the opposite wall as if he were lost in thought, and it was with a start he replied to, the • question of the Bench. "Do you call any witnesses, Mr. Selby ? " _ _ .

"Yes. Miiis Veronica Denby." The Earl started and ctoPhed painfully, the audience drew together, anu, for the moment for- getting Fanny's disclosure and Mr. Talbot Denby, turned their eyes upon the witness-box with breathless eagerness. There was some delay, then Veronica, having passed Fanny over to he mother with fervent beseechings for her forgiveness for the erring girl, entered the court and stepped into the box She was pale, but never had she looked more lovely than she did that day when she stood forth in the public eye to bear witness for the man she loved. Her eyes, calm and %weetly serene, swept the court, then rested upon Ralph with a brave, tender smile that brought the tears to the eyes of more than one of the women looking ;up at. her,

Ralph met the look with answering tenlerneB3 and love, but with something of reproach. He had prayed that this ordeal might be *spared her; but now that she had met it his heart was overrunning wird pride and gratitude. Amidst an intense stillness, Mr. Selby said gently, but in a curiously absent-minded way: "Miss Denby, you are aware that the prisoner left the Court suddenly and, as the prosecution state, without sufficient ordinary reasons. I will not ask you any questions, but let you make your statement unaided. You with the other witnesses are tpworn to tell the truth, and nothing but the truth." "Yes," she said in a low but perfectly distinct voice. Then, in accents that faltered with love and modesty, she told the story of Ralph's avowal in the arbour, her acceptance of his love, and the Earl's intervention.

"And Lord Denby "—the court stirred suddenly at the "Lord Denby "—"promised to leave the Court in answer to the Earl's appeal to him ? " "He did," she replied. -"He acted nobly, as he always has done and always will do!" There was a sound of applause, and a woman's voice AA as heard to murmur: "Ah, she loves him true, does Miss Veronica. 'Tis clear ! " Veronica heard the words, and a deep flush suffused her face; then she looked in the direc- tion of the voice, and a smile, proudly sweet, shone in her beautiful eyes: "I have 'another question to ask, Miss Denby. Were you out on the. night of the murder, the night Lord Denby left the Court ? " "I was," she answered, but with 'a somewhat startled air. • "For what purpose ? " he asked, setting his lips as if, though she was Miss Denby of Lynne Court, he could not,spare her. The blush stained her tace agian. "To see Ralph—Lord Denby," She said, her head still erect, her eyes downcast. - "To see him—why ? " "To tell him that I could not' remain at the Court if he went; to--to "—her voice broke for moment—"to ask him to marry me and take me with him."

Again there was a s,.,und of applause, and the same woman's voice said: "Ah, there's a brave woman for you! She stood by her man!" "Silence!. " cried the. clerk; but Veronica'ks eyes went to the woman again. "You did not see him ? " asked Mr. Selby. -"No "—,she suppressed a sigh—"he had gone.' "And you left Lynne Court the next morning ? " "I did, said Veronica in a low voice. "Why?" "Because I could not go on living a life of ease and luxury while he was fighting the world. 1 went to find him." ,

Gibbon did not return; presently Talbot heard a carriage drive up, and he went to the window and looked out. But it was the Earl returning in a fly. Talbot bit his lip and forced some colour into his face then went downstairs and entered the room wail an old telegram envelope in his hand. _ _

"I hope you will forgive ma for taking the carriage, sir," he said, 'but a man brought a most important telegram to the court-house, a wire from our chief. I must return at once." The Earl had sunk into his chair at Talbot's entrance his head was bent, and his eyes fixed on the carpet as if he could not bring himself to look at the man who, until lately, he had regarded as his heir and successor; for, in truth, the• old man was weighed down by the shame which should have crushed the guilty not the iznocent. He raised his head at last slowly, and looked steadily and scornfully at the sallow face. "Yoh are leaving Lynne Court for the last time, I trust," he said, his voice heavy with the shame and the exhaustion of the scene he had gone through. Talbot affected to start. "You mean—surely you cannot mean that you intend to quarrel with me, sir ? " he said, in a deeply injured tone. The Earl's eyes flashed. "Quarrel!" he echoed, his delicate nostrils expanding. "There are some men whom one does not stoop to quarrel with. But there are some one cannot suffer beneath an honest roof. Lynne Court has never yet harboured a liar and a scoundrel ! " Talbot's sallow face grew dark. "You forget whom you are addressing, my lord," he said, with 4an affectation of wounded dignity and Indignation. " Would to Heaven I could!" said the old man, with a stifled groan. "If I could only forget! But the bitterness, the shame, of remembering that you are of my blood, that you might have 7ollowed me here—here where no such villain as (ou has ever been master! But that—the chance .f your following me—has been averted, thank leaven!"

"I am not so sure," muttered Talbot; then he checked himself and sighed deeply. "You are attaching undue importance to the statement of that young woman, Fanny Mason. Surely, sir, you cannot be so ignorant of the world as not to be aware that a woman of her character is un- worthy of credit, is never, yes, never to be believed. Is it not quite a common occurre4ice for such a woman to lay the blame of her mis- fortune, misconduct, at the door of some prominent person, someone whom she may hope to blackmail, someone of higher rank t.lan herself, so that her fault may seem excusable ? " The Earl regarded him with increasing loathing and disgust. "The girl spoke the truth," he said sternly. "There was not a soul in the court who doubted her."

Talbot raised his brows. Nor would they if she had ascribed her fall from virtue to anyone else—say the prisoner," he retorted with a sneer. 'ln fact, I think she was rather foolish 'in not doing so. She left the place soon after his mysterious disappearance. He is a man of no character—in the eyes of the law, at any rate; he is charged with murder; this other, peccadillo is a slight one compared--" The Earl's eyes flashed. "You malign him ! " he cried. "He is my son—he is not capable of such baseness—"

"Grant it, sir; but why should you think me capable of betraying her ? " said Talbot, gravely. The Earl ,leant forward. "Because a thief is capable of most things." "A thief ! " Talbot retorted; then he laughed as if the accusation were too absurd to cause rtsentment. _

"Yes, a thief By what other name can you call the man who steals a will from a safe and detroys it ? " Talbot's face blanched and his lips Worked. "You saw—" escaped his lips. "I was awake—l saw ! " said the Earl. "No more. Wait! A thief is always a fool, but you are as great a one as ever existed, for you were not careful enough to see that the will you burnt was the right one—you burnt the wrong one! " Talbot's eyes flew to the safe, and he bit his lip. "There is some mistake, sir," he stammered.

THE VERDICT OF THE HEART.

By CHARLES GARVICE,

Author of "Her Heart's Desire," "In Cupid's Chains," "Just a Girl," "Nance," "The Out- cast of the Family," "A Coronet of Shame," &s.

CHAPTER XXX.

A murmur ran round the court as Fanny, with bent head, stepped into the box. What had happened to the girl? the crowd asked each other mutely. She was not only pale—that was only to be expected—but she was thin, and worn, and haggard, and she was shabbily dressed—she who had always been so neat and spruce. And why did she hang her head after the one shame- faced glance around?

In that glance her eyes had diet Talbot Denby's, but though there had been a half- pleading, assuring expression in his, hers remained sullen and woebegone. A spasm of apprehension ran through Talbot, but he told himself . that he had nothing to fear. For her own sake she would be silent as to her connection with him. And he would see her after the examination, give her money, bribe her to remain silent.

She answered the questions put to her by the prosecuting counsel in a low voice and with a piteous 'glance at Ralph's stern face, as if she wished him to know that she was giving her evidence reluctantly. She told the court how she had seen him and the deceased together on the night of the murder, had heard them quarrelling; and she turned to leave the box, when Mr. Selby rose and stopped her with a gesture. "One moment," he said, in quite a kindly manner. •"I want to ask you a few questions. Take your time and answer carefully. You know that this gentleman "—he inclined his head towards Ralph—"is charged with wilful murder, that a coroner's inquest has found him guilty, that he may be committed for trial, And that— but no, I,do not think he will be found guilty. But what I want you to realise is that your evidence is of the greatest moment, and that therefore you will speak the truth, the whole truth, and nothing but the truth." "I —I have spoken the truth," she said, almost inaudibly. "I know you have," he said, quite quietly, "and I ask you, I charge you to continue doing so. Now pray be calm, I will not distress you more than I can help," for Fanny trembled 'so much that everyone could see her shaking as she held the edge of the box. "Now, the detective Who was sent to find you found you staying at a coffee-house. You were not in a situation ? " She shook her head. "No, sir." • Talbot, who had listened with an unmoved face, stirred in his seat to attract her attention and shot a warning glance at her. "Why did you leave your situation, the one you went up _froni here to fill ? " "I —I was not happy. I did not like it," she answered in a low voice. "Try to speak a little louder," said Mr. Selby, encouragingly. "You have nothing to fear while you speak the truth, the whole truth, you know. Where was that situation ? "

"In—in St. John's Wood," came the reply, still less audibly. Mr. Selby's eyes glinted, and he scanned her keenly. "St. John's Wood," he said, very softly and soothingly. "What was the address, and the name of your mistress?" Fanny's lips twitched, and she raised her oyes appealingly to the Bench. "Am I obliged—must I ans, er ? " she asked, piteously. "I've told all I w, and sorry 1 am to have to tell it. I—l woulln't do Mr. Ralph an injury: he knows I wouldn't! He was always kind to me. Oh. I wish I'd never gone! Must I answer?"

"You must answer," said Lord Saintsbury, gravely. "Yes, why should you be afraid?" said Mr. Selby, gently. She gave them the name of the house, then faltered and paused. _ _ _

"The name of the mistress?" asked Mr. Selby, gravely. "There—there was none," she said at last. was my own house—it was not a situation."

There was a stir in the court, and the people looked from one to the other in surprise and dawning suspicion. Talbot glanced towards thQ magistrates' entrance as if the idea of escape had flashed across his mind, but he checked it tuu, sat motionless, his sallow face set in an im- passive calm. ' "So you did not go to a situation. Did yon tell your people that you were going to a house of your own ? " "No," came from the trembling lips,

Mr. 'Selby paused. He knew tliat fie was going to reveal a story of sin and shame, but he could not help himself; his 'client's life was at stake, and the truth must be extracted from her.

"You were not rich enough to own a house— who took it for you ? " She covered her face with her hands and drooped in the box as if weighed down with shame.

"Oh, I need not answer, I need not tell!" she moaned.

"You must answer," he said, rather sternly. "You lived in this house at St. John's Wood as the mistress, who was the—master?"

There was a,profound silence, broken only by the breathing of the excited crowd. "Come," said Mr. Selby, "tell us quickly. The sooner you answer the sooner you will be allowed to go. Wait! If their worships will permit, you shall write the name of the gentle- man who took the house for you."

The magistrates conferred a moment, then Lord Saintsbury said: "We think that will be sufficient."

The clerk took a piece of paper to thp box an? put a pen in the shaking hand, and Fanny, after a glance towards that end .of the Bench where Mr. Talbot Denby sat, wrote a name. The dui- took the paper from her and handed it to Mr. Selby. He did not start as he read the name did not look up for a moment, but his eyes glinted as they had done when Fanny mentioned "St. John's Wood," and his lips .tightened. He stood for quite a minute, his eyes fixed on the paper, then he handed it up to the Bench. The magis- trates' heads drew together over the paper, they exchanged glances, and their eyes were directed, as-if .by.one impulse, to Mr. Talbot Denby. Not a word was spoken for a moment or two, then Mr. Selby said in a.grave voice: .._

"You -swear that— this is the name of the gentleman ? " s.

- Fanny inclined her head. "Why did you leave St. John's Wood ? " asked Mr. Selby.

Her fw-e worked and her voice was broken by sobs.

"We—we quarrelled. He—he told me he, was tired of me, and—ad I went. He 'offered me money, but I wouldn't t4e it. I Imid some, and I—l took a room in the coffee-house where they found me. Oh, I've toldtyou all! Let me gity please; please let me go! Mr. Talbot knows it's the truth!"

Unwittingly she had let out the name. There was a movement in the crowd, it swayed to and fro, and, as every eye sprang at him as it were, Talbot rose to his feet. His face was white to he lips, but he curved them with an insolent sneer.

"I take it that the girl wrote my name on the piper," he said slowly, contemptuously. "I will not stoop to deny the accusation. It is not the first time a loose woman has endeavoured to shelter herself behind an innocent person." , Fanny's face burnt with passionate indignation. 'Does he—does he deny it!" she gasped, as much in amazement ag indignation. "He knows it's true! He persuaded me to go with him, he promised me—and I believed him, believed him ! Oh, it's Ow truth ! " She turned to Mr. Selby, the Bench, with clasped hands. "Look at him, look at his face! It was Mr. Talbot Denby who took me away ! " _ _ _

The crowd-surged towarde the spot where he stood, and muttered threateningly, for Fanny Mason had been the belle of the locality, and many of the young men present had paid court to her. As they looked from her to him they remembered-her as a pretty light-hearted girl; and cow Ralph stood sternly rezardina the scoundrel— his cousin men swittly nis eyes went tnwards Veronica.. CHAPTER X.ILIZI.

"Drive quickly, please ! " Talbot said to the coachman, and as the high-spirited horses tore down the road Talbot tried ts> recover his self- possession. He realised that he had been unwise in not returning to the court-house, in flying like—like a criminal; but it was too late to go hack now. He, must reach the house and get to London; he could invent some excuse for his sudden departure. In London he would have time .to think, to face the situation and meet it.

After all, there was no- danger of the truth being known. He would live to see Ralph Farrringdon hanged. He got out of the carriage quickly and went up the, stairs. At the top Gibbon, who had been looking down at him with a keen intentness in his dull,. colourless eyes; moved slowly across the -3orrid or.

"Is that you, Gibbon?" said Talbot. "Pack my porttnanteau and order a carriage—a close earriage—to take us to the station. I have been summoned to town on—on important business." "Yes, sir," said Gibbon. 'There's no train Lill the 4.30, sir," he added, in his toneless voice.

Talbot, as he .entered his room, turned on him

savagely. "Obey my orders," he said, harshly, and be quick about it You-----you move about the place like a black slug." Gibbon raised his head and looked at his master's back with a twitch .of his lips that was like a mute snarl, then he went downstairs. A few minutes later Talbot, who was pacing up c.nd down his room vainly, trying to recover his usual calmness, to assure himself that he was in no peril, heard the man•packing in the adjoining room. Gibbon was a long time about the task, but presently he dame into the bedroom. "Your things are packed, sir," he said; "and the dog-cart is waiting. Talbot faced round upon him passionately; for there is no passion so intense, so ungovernable, ( as fear.

"You senseless hound r he said. "I told, you a close carriage, a brougham I Go and order it! You'll leave my service a month from to-day, and will take with you the character I had 'with von. Yes. this day month: it will not be very cong after that before you find yourself in gaol, I've no doubt. Get out of my sight!" • As Gibbon went .out Talbot fell to pacing up and down again. If he could only get away before the Earl returned! But half-an-hour yarned and

. . The Earl smiled a terrible smile of merciless cynicism. "Yea, it was a mistake. But no matter. You are going. • It is long since I offered up a prayer, but I pray now that I may never see your face again." Talbot's dark eyes flashed savagely, then he cast them down and sighed deeply. 'You are unjust, sir.; you wrong me," he said. "I will write to you from London—l dare not stay-and I will ask you to keep an s open mind until you have hear.l my explanation, my defence." The Earl raised his hand and pointed to the door; and Talbot, with another sigh, passed out. The brougham was waiting, a footman opened the door for him, and Talbot got in. Gibbon mounted the box, and the carriage drove to the station.

"I'm afraid there's more than an hour to wait, sir," said Gibbon,.as he stood beside the carriage door when it pulled up at the steep flight of wooden stairs leading to the station. Talbot glanced at his Watch. "Get the tickets," he said, as if he Were speaking to a dog, and a cur at that. He followed the man up the stairs, and lighting a cigarette paced up and down the platform, his head erect, though his face was pale and heavy; in fact, he loo.ked as he had often looked when the luck had gone against him in some gambling hell. After a time he grew tired of the monotonous pacing, and, on reaching the end of the platform, he stepped off and walked beside the line, scarcely conscious of what he was doing. No one inter- fered with him—though he passed a -porter and the signalman seated on the steps of his box— and, indeed, he was in no danger, for the embankment was broad. It was also a very high one and after about half-a-mile it led to the viaduct which, at its erection, had caused quite an excitement in the neighbourhood, and was still regarded with pride by the simple country folk who Considered it a marvel of engineering skill. Talbot leant with folded arms against the low stone wall and looked into the valley which the arches spanned. But it may safely be said . that he did not see the pretty coombe, with its beech and oak trees, and its silvery stream running peacefully between banks Dl' fern and gorse. So far down seemed the sheep and cattle that browsed beside the brook, that they appeared dway:ed and as if in a "bird's-eye" picture. ,Talbot, though he was staring at it, saw nothing of the loveliness of:the view; he was looking at the mental picture of the slim, white-faced old ...nan pointing to the door, at a still more un- welcome vision—that of the hated Ralph Farringdon reigning at Lynne Court. But no, no! Ralph Farringdon would be hanged! He was awakened from his reverie by a shadow falling across the coping of the bridge, and, turning his eyes, he saw that it was Gibbon. "I. told you to wait at the station," said Talbot in a low voice. "Go back ! "

Gibbon still approached, so that his shadow fell athwart his master's.

"I wanted to speak to you, Mr. Talbot," he said. "I wanted to tell you that I'm not coming up to town with you." Talbot eye 4 him haughtily. "Go back. You are coming Nfrith me. I have need of you, strange as it may seem. You gaol-bird! Did you hear what I said ? "

Gibbon nodded and leant against the Wall, his hands thrust into his pockets, his colourless eyes fixed on Talbot's furious face. Talbot thought he was drunk.

"Oh, yes, I heard," said Gibbon, coolly. "But I'm not coming. I'm going back to the Court to Lord Lynborough. I'm going to make a clean breast of it."

"You've been drinking again, you beast!" said Talbot between his teeth. "You are right; you are not fit to accompany me. Go, get out of my sight! I'll find some means of punishing you.

- Gibbon spat on the ground and looked before him as if he had not heard his dismissal. "Yes, I'm going to tell them all about it." "All about. what ? " demanded Talbot, 'sur- prised into the question by the man's audacity. "The murder, said Gibbon, as coolly as before. Talbot did not start, was not, indeed, startled. "What do you know about it ? " he said, with a kind of impatient contempt. "What have you got into your bead, you besotted fool !" _ _ .

know all about it," said GibbOn, turning his eyes to his master for th,e first time. "Nihy, I was thereiall the time."

Talbot's hands closed on.the edge of the coping, and the blue shy speitied to 4iin to turn a blood red.

"You—were--?" He could say no more. for his tongue seemed to 1111X0 thickened and, tilled his mouth.

Gibbon nodded. "Yes," he said,. with a twist Of the lips that Was a hideous travesty of a smile. "I followed you that night. I thought you .were up to something, Mr. Talbot.. I watched you that evening as I've watched you often and often before. Oh. I know what you keen in the safe in

"I think not," said Talbot "You are not sunh a fool as to tell this story, produce that pocket- book when, by holding your tongue, just holding your tongue, as you linve done, and giving me the book, you can earn a couple of thousand pounds."

To his amazement, his terror, Gibbon laughed. "Too late!" he said, coolly. "You weren't in court to the end—they told me how you bolted --and didn't hear Miss Veronica swear that she saw you that night; you don't know that Mr. Selby—ah, he's a. sharp 'un, he is !—has got his suspicions. "

While he had been speaking, slowly, taunt- ingly, as if he were enjoying every word as be sent it home, Talbot's quick- brain was working. And this was the line it was taking, with that swiftness, that marvellous brecision whioi is as miraculous as the coming of spring, the growth of a grain of corn, the flowering of the daisy. The man, this man-fiend who was torturing him, was in possession of the pocket-book. If he were found, dead say, with that book in his pocket, who would doubt that he was the murder? Still more swiftly, acutely, the trained .brain worked.

The man was sittinrr on the wall. If he fell Talbot could say that th,, man had confessed the murder, had tried to make terms, that he, Talbot, struggled with him. With a low cry, the guttural cry of a tiger springing on his prey, Talbot Hum, himself on him. But, quick as the .attack haebeen, Gibbon had been warned by the murderous flash of the eyes, mid ha stiffened himself, and, flinging out his arms, threw them round Talbot. But he, Gibbon, had been taken at a disadvantage, and he could , only manage, with every bit of his strength exerted, to keep his back straight; he could not get his feet to the ground. Talbot seized the a,.lvint-,age and pressed with al his force. until Gibbon's back was nearly broken by the ti_Trillie strain. Then, With the. suddeenes born of desrair, Gibbon hoisted his sssailant on to a level with himself.- They swayed for a moment --a terrible moment I—on ;he top of the wail, then, with an awful cry which seemed to rime from hoth lips at the same instant, they fell over, loeked in each other's sloe, to the peaceful v•tfley beneath.

It is a very long time since.there were so many busy and happy children in Warrington.The cause of their con- tentedness has been play,as our readers may have noticed. Every schoolchild has a pleasing toy which they have been spinning to their hearts' content; a beautiful wind- mill which when in motion ia a dream of colour. The donors of these pretty things are the Woods' Great Peppermint Cure Company who are extensively adver- tising their preparation in this Town at the moment. Their .methods arc certainly instructive in the highest degree as they work upon an educational basis, both on cojour and literature. They claim that New Zealanders swear by Peppermint •Core as a certain remedy for Coughs and Colds. It will be interesting to, hear what the All Blacks say about it when they reach Warrington

If you'd listened to me the other day When 1 asked for the money—not half enough, Mr. Talbot, sir, not half enough !—it you'd treated me like a man instead of a dog, I'd have held my tongue, and you'd have been safe; for they say the evidence was strong enough to hang that young gentleman—l mean Lord Denby; but you was a fool. You've always treated me as if I was the dirt beneath your feet—worse ! But I'm thinking it's my turn now, Mr. Talbot! The man's garrulity had given Talbot time to recover from the shock, to collect his scattered senses. He laughed scornfully. "My good man, you talk like an idiot who has just awakened from a dream—nightmare! And you thought to frighten me! Bah! Who do you think would believe this cock-and-bull storz. invented by a gaol-bird, a convict, who happened to bear his master a grudge?" Gibbon appeared unmoved. "That's right enough," he said, "but I've got some evidence. There was the clothes you told me to brush. I've kept them, they're all over mould and moss."

"I fell intci a ditch.- said Talbot, quickly, contemptuously. "You might have worn thew yourself. Ah—you see! Gibbon eyed him with a reflection of the contempt in Talbot's eyes. "Oh, stow it!" he said, scornfully. "I've got the pocket-book with th certificates! Talbot' 13 face grew white and his eyes expanded. Gibbon nodded. "Yes. That touches you, Mr. Talbot, doesn't it? I got it out of the man's pocket while you went for the spade. Remember? Talhet remembered. Was there any detail of that hideous deed that did not haunt him perpetually? "Ir4e, got 'em here, 4r," said Gibbon, strikine his breast coat-poeket. "They'll help the Sotini gentleman get his rights as well as hang you, Mr. Denby." Talbot stood as if turned to stone; then suddenly he said: "My man you have over- reached yourself! Do you know—but, of course, you do not—that if I committed—if I "—his tongue bothered him again—" if I was guilty you, too, are in danger? You concealed the thing you are, as they call it, an accessory after the fact."

Gibbon moved his 'lips into a smile.' "Yes, 1 know," he said, coolly; 'but I'm going to make my bargain with his lordship the Earl, and tlw police. I'm going to torn King's evidence: ever heard of that, Mr. Talbot? Oh, I may be a dog, and a gaol-bird, but I'm not so ignorant as you think ! " As he spoke he seated himself on the edge of the low wall and swung his legs, eyeing his ,master with an air of gloating triumph.

Talbot looked over the Man's head with an absent •gaze, with eyes that saw 'nothing, but— but what? Was it a scaffold, a tall figure, hideously like himself, with a white cap drawn over the face ?- He shuddered and passed his hand over his eyes. "See here, Gibbon," he said in a low voice. "You have been a faithful servant to me."

U' You houhd you drunken beast'—' you gaol- bird ! ' " murmured Gibbon, his eyes lighting up vindictively. "A faithful servant, and I do not think you would do me an injury." Gibbon drew his thin lips into a grin. "Tile least injury I would do you is to bang you," he said. "And I will ! "
