capture the society, and prevent it from doing any ' mischief by interfering with administration and discipline. Lord CLAUD HAMILTON'S reasoning, in some places, shows how even an able man may persuade himself of the truth of what he desires to believe. He talks of the disastrous effects of the recognition of trade unionism, which will interfere with administration and discipline, telling us that this has been the universal experience. If that be the case, how do all the other industries manage to exist? How does the Post Office keep up its efficiency? Have the miners ruined the coal industry? Why, in the case of the miners, where trade unionism has been recognised for many years, we found not long ago that masters and men combined to put presaure on Parliament to take offthe export duty. They may have been right or wrong, but it is safe to say the masters there would never have succeeded in getting the duty taken off had it not been for the active co-operation of the men. That is but one example, and many great masters of industry tell us that they would much rather bargain I with the leaders of a men's union than not, and that, as a fact, disputes are less frequent. Interference with administration and discipline has not been the universal experience of trade unionism. It is to be regretted that Lord CLAUD HAMILTON should have designated the demand for recognition as a Socialist plot, for that is what his manifesto means. Nothing has done more to assist the growth of Socialism than the attacks which have lately been made upon it for political purposes. If Socialism should ever become a real force the cause will lie at the door, not of those sober minded working men who are demanding recognition and asking for collective bargaining, but of those who thought there was some momentary party advantage to be gained by designating all as Socialists who happen to believe in Social Reform. The directors may think that they have only to shout," Yah, Socialist !" and that then they will win. Tbey obviously do think they can win, and possibly they may; but it will be at the price of a terrible upset of trade, of much suffering among women and children, and of a big eventual growth of that very " ism " of which they now pretend to be afraid. We hope better counsels will yet prevail, and that Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE will be able to do what the country expects of him, bring the parties to terms without a fight.

After all ther 3 are only to be THE ELECTIONS. two contests in Warrington's A BREWERS' BATTLE. nine wards on November

Ist. Plot and counter-plot have resulted in nothing tangible since our last issue, and the sole contestants in the arena will be Councillor Wilding and Mr. W. Rogers (Town Hall Ward) and Councillor Clegg and Mr. W. B. Roberts (Howley Ward). These four gentlemen will be the cynosure of all eyes, so to speak, till about 8.30 on Friday evening when we anticipate that the two challengers will gracefully retire from view. There is just one point of interest about the elections, and that is that the two gentlemen who oppose the retiring Councillors are both connectod with one trade, the trade. Moreover the only retiring Conservative, Councillor Fulton- Smith ccimes under the same classification, so that the Conservative Party in Warrington is repre- sented at this year's municipal elections simply and solely by the brewers and sellers of beer. Our Tory friends are evidently being led by the nose with grave danger of a sad discolouration of that organ. There is no secret of the fact that the brewers all over the country are chafing at legis- lative restrictions and using all possible means to secure political influence. The municipal elec- tions offer them a fair field and in Warrington at any rate the chance has been taken with both hands. We have no fear that the public will be gulled into believing that the representatives of the trade are as well fitted as other folk for municipal representation. The dictum is generally accepted now-a-days that the fewer brewers and publicans on a Town Council and the better for pure and efficient adminis- tration. We mean no offence to these gentle- man as individuals—they may have excellent personal qualifications—but the public welfare is so dependent on the strictest surveillance of the drink trade, that anything more than a nominal representation of the trade on the local authority becomes a menace. There are already four gentlemen on the Town Council whose livelihood is bound up with the sale of strong drink, viz., Aldermen Bolton and Evans, and Councillors Fulton Smith and Chantler. The proportion, out of 36 members, is quite enough for one industry even were it the most harmless. To make the numbers into six on November Ist would be a grave error into which we have no feat of the electors falling. They will, we hope, reject in unmistakable fashion the candidature of Messrs. W. B. Roberts and Rogers on Friday next

Five retiring Aldermen are THE ALDERMEN. said to be quaking in

their shoes, viz., Messrs. Smethurst, Forshaw, Evans Francomb and Bolton. Perhaps we should have said four, for Alderman Francomb does not seek re-election. The fate of the others lies "in the lap of the gods." There might be changes with distinct advantage. We have no love for the custom which ensures an Alderman being always an Alderman. He should come before the ratepayers at the end of his term of six years, and receive the hall-mark of fitness. When the new Council meets in November there will be, therefore, five Aldermanic vacancies to be filled. A contest for the honours would show a healthy tendency in our civic life, and might very easily lead to an improvement in the personnel of the select few. The idea, cunningly fostered by a contemporary, that the Liberals and Non-Traders had combined, to throw some of the old aldermen out and put in, among others, Councillor Pemberton, is absurd on the face of it, The Non-Traders abhor party compacts, and have always made it perfectly clear that they seek no honour but that of well-doing. They may influence the aldermanic election, of 1 course, but not on behalf of one of their number. I Moreover, they decided some time ago to take no I action till after the nominations for Municipal candidates. So far as we are aware neither party has moved in the matter of the aldermen at all. When they do there will he time to talk, and probably something to talk about.

Liberalism achieved great

LIBERALISM IN THE things in the Knut-sford KNIMFORD DIVISION. division at the last election and the enthu- siasm which resulted in the triumphant return of Mr. A. J. King, who has the distinction of being the first Liberal representative of the constitu ency, shows no signs of waning. On tile contrary the progressive cause continues to make rapid strides. Mr. King has already proved himself an energetic and conscientious member and during the recess he is doing his best to get into touch with his constituents in all parts of the large and scattered division. He has recently held several very encouraging meetings and on Thursday even- ing he motored to Stretton. where a numerous company listened to his informing and thoughtful address on the work of the Government. Mr. King had no difficulty in showing that Sir Henry Catnpbell-Bannerman and his supporters in the House are thoroughly in earnest in their efforts for social amelioration and the large number of useful measures which have found their way to the Statute Book during the last two years afford proof that the workers can have full confidence in the Government. The interests of the agriculturalist as well as of the town dweller and the seaman have been carefully studied, and Mr. King enumerated Acts which are bound to be of great

benefit to the farming community in particular. He was questioned on the subject of the "big loaf " and gave a very conclusive answer to the heckler who insinuated that subsequent events had not squared with the Government's election pledges. Mr. Fred Horne, the tenant farmer candidate for Ludlow Division, accompanied Mr. King and in a stirring and convincing address dealt with the advantages which had been con- ferred upon farmers and other rural dwellers by the Government. The Land Tenure Act he aptly described as the farmer's charter, while the Small Holdings and Allotments Act will do much to improve the prospects of those who are destined to work on the land.

After a lengthy discussion THE FEEDING OF on Monday the Education SCHOOL CHILDREN. Committee decided almost

unanimously to request the Town Council to adopt the Provision of Meals Act 1906. The School Attendance Committek's report on the estimated cases of children who from various causes came to school improperly fed was presented, and we note that it bears out the figures we gave a month ago. Altogether, out of some 14,000, there are 277 children attending school insufficiently fed, 191 suffering through poverty and 86 from neglect. The bulk of the children were found at four schools which cater for the inhabitants of a poor quarter of the town., Obviously the problem confronting the local education authorities is not serious becaase of its immensity. Nevertheless it is one which must entail most earnest consideration before the Act is brought into operation. "Interference with parental responsibilty " has grown into a stock phrase applicable to this question, but it is not without meaning. Fortunately there is no indi- cation in Wareington of a tendency to a grand- motherly provision of free breakfasts wholesale. When the time comes the individual cases will be considered on their merits, and the parents of the children who are wantonly neglected will find in the Act not a means for lessening their responsibilities, but an instrument for enforcing proper parental care. The Act may prove a bless- ing to poor people, and one not so hedged round with degrading circumstance as the help of the Union authorities. The children themselves, whether neglected or poor or both, have a right to State patronage. Healthy child-life is the root of national prosperity both material and mental, and if the Education scheme can be thus extended so as to equip the bodies as well as the brains of the young for the battle of life it will approach more nearly to perfection.

The German—or to be pre- SCANDALS OF A cise the Prussian—Court COURT. scandals are not pleasant

reading. We have no intention of judging Count Kuno Moltke or his sccuser, Herr Harden. The libel action which the General is bringing against the journatist is not of a very savoury character, and, whatever its out- come, is not likely to reflect much credit upon the circle led by Prince Eulenberg and Count Moltke. With its political significance, however, practically the whole of Europe is concerned. The proceedings of the Eulen- berg group of courtiers were brought to an end some time ago upon their becoming known to the Emperor. Their influence was wholly pernicious. It aimed at establishing in the Imperial mind a conception of the throne which even in 'Gerinany would be impossible. William 11. has been credited with holding to the doctrine of the Divine right of kings; but with its development, as proposed by the Eulenberg set, he had nothing •to do, and his mark of disapproval as soon as the private ends of that set were revealed to him was sharp and complete. The Kaiser has had many trials since he came to the throne. Prince Hohenlohe revealed the intrigues which beset him, and which seem indigenous to German Qourt life. Perhaps his prompt and emphatic Xction with regard to the particula7 coterie which Prince Eulenberg and Count Kuno Moltke con- trolled will have the effect of 'clearing the Court life of some of this tendency to undue influence being centred in certain cliques.

No one will deny that Lord THE RAILWAY CLAUD HAMILTON'S maui- DISPUTE. festo on behalf of his Own

and other railway corn ' panics is an extremely able document. But it is ' also extremely significant, and in some respects very unwise. Why he should have dragged in the fear of Socialism is very difficult to see, for if there is one person more than another who has fought Socialism among working men in this country . that person is surely Mr. RICHALD BELL. All the world knows that he is a vigorous and convinced anti-Socialist, and those who fear Socialism, if they are sincere in that fear, would do well to range on their side men of the strong personality and decided views Of Mr. BELL. Lord CLAITD HAMILTON'S letter, however, seems to us to show that the directors now desire a strike, that they think it would be short and sharp, that they can win and smash the power of the Amalgamated Society for the next twenty years. If the letter does not mean that, it is a peace of the most magnificent bluff ever written; but we fear it does mean that the companies are determined now to have it out with the men. To the plain man it is very difficult to see what harm could come to the companies by recognition. We are told that the majority of the men are not in the society, but that the moment recognition took place that would mean forcing the remainder,. whom the directors desire to protect, into the society. Now if these men are so loyal to the companies, surely the best possible thing the companies could do would be t) let them

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Window-boxes have been much in evidence this summer in certain parts of the town. Unfortunately the climatic conditions of July and August were not favourable to outdoor plants and they suffered in con- sequence. It is to be boped that their owners will shortly plant bulbs in the boxes, so that we may have a good show in the spring. The crocus is, perhaps, the best plant for this purpose.

• - Dr. Joseph, having resigned the position of medical officer to the Charity Tent No. 11 of the Independent Order of Rechabites after 26 years' services, was on Saturday evening presented with a fountain pen by the members. The doctor has made as many friends as cures during this long period, and his popularity with the Rechabites is not to be measured by the presentation which came so spontaneously. * * _ _ _

There were some oratorical gems at Monday's meet- ing of the Education Committee during the discussion on the provision of meals for school-children. One speaker pointed out that the new Act would not give the authorities "cart blank" to feed any children they might bring in from the streets ; another urged that the " physic " of the rising generation was the great question to consider; while a third waxed eloquent on the value of the information supplied by the" 'ed teachers."

• 0 It is encouraging to hear how the idea of such societies as the Beautiful Warrington Society is spreading. We hear of one that has just been started in Sheffield. The Warrington society has also received a letter from America enquiring about the work,as they hope to do something on the same lines. Lastly from Leeds comes an intimation of an Exhibition to be held in November illustrating the architecture, gardening and general arrangement of villages. All this is good but an income of ;£lOO, a year for the Warrington Society would be better!

- . The Tory heckler was en evidence at the Liberal meeting at Stretton on Thursday evening. He kept up a running commentary on the speeches, asked questions, and made efforts to address the meeting. He was treated in a very good-humoured way, and was responsible for one amusing incident. On the school wall is a clock which had stopped, the fingers pointing throughout the evening to 9.40. When the heckler interrupted Mr. Horne that speaker genially replied " If the gentleman will wait until that clock strikes ten he can speak as much as he likes !" Eventually the questioner took his departure with the remark that "he must get a drink before ten."

For cool cheek the following official Conservative query addressed to retiring Warrington Councillors will take some beating: "Is it your intention if returned unopposed to vote on the 9th of November for new men to replace the whole or any of the retiring aldermen ? " Except that the retiring aldermen are all Conservatives, we do not see what business the Con- servative Association has to ask such a question. We hope most of the Councillors showed their resent- ment at thus having the pistol put to their heads. Unfortunately we know that one or two Councillors temporised. Some of them are very unstable at election times. * *

The Junior Liberal Association is an energetic and enterprising body and the season just inaugurated promises to be highly successful. The first half of the syllabus has been arranged and is as follows : November 4th, "The value and significance of the last Hague conference," Mr. H. Gray ; November 11th, "Local Municipal Questions," Mr. Councillor Hawthorn; November 10th, "A night with the law and the lawyer," Mr. E. England; November 25th, " Social Evolution and current politics," Mr. R. E. Burtt ; December 2nd, "Licensing Reform," Mr. James Jameson, secretary of the Temperance Legisla- tion League; December 9th, "Socialism and. Liberalism," Mr. R. Pierce Williams ; December 16th Mr. R. Pierce Williams will reply to criticisms on his paper. General regret is felt at the resignation of of Mr. Harold Gray, one of the hon. secs, who was a ,pioneer:bf the Association and has done yeomen service in its interests,

* * Visitors to the Coloniel Fruit Show organised by the Royal Horticultural Society cannot fair to have noticed the Calabash tobacco-pipes that are a feature of some of the exhibits and even now in tobacconists' shops. Writing about their preparation in the "Kew Bulletin," Professor Pearson says : " The cultivation is carried on in various parts of the Colony. The plants are watered once a week, but otherwise require little attention. The fruits are produced from December to May. The curve of the stalk-end of the fruit, from which the pipe is made is produced naturally; but, if necessary, it is increased by subjecting the fruit to longitudinal pressure when it is half grown. This is accomplished either by standing it in an erect position so that its weight is borne by the stalk-end, or by a suitable arrangement of stones placed in contact with its lower half as it lies prone on the ground. As it ripens the green colour of the fruit changes to a light yellow. After cutting it is dried in the sun."

* * * Monday was the one hundred and second annversary of Trafalgar Day and once more the youth of Britain will bear special testimony to the memory of their country's greatest sailor. When his parents proposed that Horatio Nelson should be sent to the sea as his profession, his uncle, Captain Suckling, of H.M.S. Raisonnable, 64 guns, responded to the suggestion with the remark, "What has poor Horatio done, who is so weak, that he, above, all the rest, should be sent to rough it out at sea? But let him come; and the first time we go into action a cannon-ball may knock off his head, and provide for him at once." The future hero of Trafalgar was only twelve years old.. when he responded to this somewhat chilling invita- tion and joined his uncle's ship. The lad has left on record how unhappy he was on his journey to Chatham, when the Raisonnable was lying in the Medway. He was sent alone from London on the coach, and lost his way, and when he did eventually reach the ship, his uncle was absent, and no one expected his arrival.

* * Socialists are claiming that they will have 1,200 candidates at the coming municipal elections in England. This, (says the London correspondent of the "Liverpool Post and Mercury ") is probably an exaggeration, but there will be a great number of Socialist candidates there is no doubt whatever. By Socialist candidates are meant not merely mentwith popular and Radical sympathies, but men with distinct and pronounced Socialist views. The policy which they advocate includes the efficient administration of the Feeding of School Children Act and the Unemployed Act, a municipal coal supply, a municipal milk supply, the municipilisation of the drink traffic, and the transfer of all public services to the munici- pality. Another item in their programme is the better housing of the working classes. Some of the items in their programme are sound enough, but others can only be carried out at a vast expenditure. Should the Socialists obtain a majority on any municipal body the unfortunate ratepayers will have ts pay the piper.

0 * * It is calculated that the working days of all the workmen in the United Kingdom amount to 3,000,000,000 in a year, and last year trade disputes caused the loss of a little over three million days, one- thousandth of the whole, or one-third day per head. According to the annual report of the Labour Depart- ment of the Board of Trade, the number of workpeople affected by all disputes was 217,773, or over 30 per cent. above the average of the previous ten years, and more than double the average of the previous three years. No fewer than 50,000 workers struck on some principle of trade unionism, and in only 773 instances did they fail to make good their claim, a fact which suggests some bearing on the present railway crisis. An interesting section deals with the work of the permanent conciliation and arbitration boards, which dealt with 39 cases, in which 7,885 workpeople were affected, the greatest number previously so dealt with in any one year being 29, but by the conclusion of matters initiated in the previous year the figures are increased to 46 cases and 14,366 workers, a number far greater than the average. of other years.

Hundreds of people have had a finger trapped in a railway carriage door,but few have been so fortunate as a plaintiff at the Warrington CountyConrt on Thursday who obtained £25 damages from the London and North-Western Railway Company. The case is of special interest, for it seems that the plaintiff rose to shut the door after the train had started, and the door was of course banged to at the psycological moment, with the usual result. The Company's lawyer argued loftily that he had no case to answer, as plaintiff had taken upon himself the functions of a railway servant in rising to shut the door. Judge Shiress Will took a different view, and decided that plaintiff's act was a reasonable one for the safety of himself and other passengers, seeing that the train had started, and gave a judgment in his favour. The damages had been previously agreed upon in case of a verdict. Mr. Lambert, for the Rail- way Company, desired a stay of execution, but it was refused. We should not be surprised however, if the Company takes the case further. They will not 'like having to admit liability for the number of crushed fingers incurred in this way each year. If the fault is ever the Company's, we should imagine it always is, for no one pokes a finger wilfully into such a tight corner.

Whatever the value of c—onsistency in politics may be, it is certainly a virtue which the Premier possesses in a pre-eminent degree. It was said of him in " The Daily News," on one occasion, that " Mr. Campbell- Bannerman has said, fewer foolish things than any public man of his time." I am reminded (says X.Y.Z.) of the statement by a record prepared by the " Dunfermline Journal," in view of his speech there this evening of Sir Henry's utterances as member for the Stirling Burghs. After reading it, one may add that the Premier has probably eaten fewer of his. words than any other living statesman. sti 0 Notwithstanding the unkindness of the weather, the lbazaar and fete held at Hill Cliffs during June on behalf of the Infirmary Extension Fund was a splendid success. At the final meeting of the committee held at the Town Hall on Wednesday it was reported that £2,271 15s. id. was to be handed over to the fund —a figure which speaks volumes for the energy. of the workers. This brings the total amount received for the extension up-to-date to £14,675, so that the goal is well within sight. The Chairman, however, pointed out that sundry extras had had to be incurred in alterations to the existing buildings, and that if £15,500 were raised, it would not exceed the require- ments of the case, so that the generously-disposed have still a chance!

Band of Hope _Union.

The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Band of Hope Union was held at Gregory's Cafe on Thursday, Mr. W. H.Sherburne presiding over a good attendance. The report of the delegates of the United Kingdom Band of Hope Union Conference at Dublin was read and adopted, together with the report of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union. The Warrington and District Union has sent out circulars to all the churches in the town asking them to strictly observe Temperance Sunday on November 24th and they wonld provide speakers for the occasion. Letters of apology for non-attendance were read from the Rev. C. Harvey-Cook, Miss Bessie Dutton and the Rev. J. F. Matthews. The following officers were elected for thee ensuing year: President, Mrs. James Dodds vice-presidents, Revs. J. S. Hughes, J. Yonge ; H. Wall, F. Vaughan, C. Harvey Cook, J. F. Matthews, T. Miller, J. B. Buglass, Mrs. Simpson, Mis, Bessie Dutton, Messrs. A. Mounfield, W. H. Sherburns H. Clatworthy, J. J. Firth, Ben Few, H. Thornton„ E. J. Guest, and Mrs. Fyldes ; hon. treasurer, Mr. R. Pearce Williams; hon. secretary, Mr. F. 0-andy ; assistant secretaries, Messrs. H. Woolley and John Muir; auditors, Messrs. Fernley and Fellows; stock- takers, Messrs. Fernley, Fellows and Whitham ; representatives Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union, Messrs, W. Wright and B. Warburton; accompanist, Mr. Albert Clatworthy ; conductor, Mr. A. Spruce.

Railway Official's Retirement. Mr. J. J. Bilsbrough, goods agent and dockmaster of the London and Nortlr-Western Railway Company. at Widnes, is about to retire from business, conse- quent upon severe lameness from which he has suffeted for the past two years. Mr. Bilsbrough commenced his business career in 1860, when he joined the staff of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway Company at Preston. In 1886 he joined the staff of the London and North-Western Railway Company and for over thirteen years was engaged at the district goods manager's office, Warrington. He is very largely esteemed by his colleagues and all who have been associated with him in business. PROVISIONS are much dearer. Hams, Bacon, Lard and Butter are dearer.—Advt. Penny Popular Concerts. Hams, Bacon, Lard and Batter are dearer.—Advt. Cruelty to a Dog. John Massey, 7, Wilderspool-road, Warrington, was fined £2 at the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday, ' for causing a dog to be cruelly ill-treated by inciting another dog to worry it on October 6. - Defendant, who was represented by Mr. Jenkins, pleaded not guilty. On the morning in question a man named Edward Plumb went down to some stables at Arpley with a little dog, when • the defendant's dog rushed at it, but subsequently loosed it. A witness named Stangle remarked that the defendant's dog ought to be shot, when the defen- dant brought his dog out and said, "Shoot it." The animal again got hold of the little dog, and although defendant's son was endeavouring to separate them defendant said, " Let them alone ; let him kill it they have no business to bring the dog here." For the defence several witnesses swore that the defendant did not incite the dog to worry the other animal. For Chronic Chest Complaint, Woods' Great • Peppermint Cure. 1/11, 2/9.—Advt.

THE ALDERMANIC QUESTION.

Alderman Tinnion Repudiates a Party Compact.

Speaking at the Liberal Club last evening, Alder- man Tinmon said the Liberal party had been charged with entering into a compact with another party in the council to capture the Town Hall. "As a member of the Liberal party, as one who should know if any- thing of that kind has been discussed and undertaken by the Liberal party, I repudiate the charge entirely. Vire have not attempted to do anything of the sort. I do not hesitate to say that the Conservative party, if they had had a similar opportunity, would have em- braced it, I think, with both hands. They would have captured the Town Hall, because they have been try- ing to do it for a very considerable time."

===== 0002601_19071026_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
POINTS FROM THE ELECTION ADDRESSES.

Mr. G T. Wilding in his address to the electors of Town Hall Ward says he is an avowed Non-Trader--- a member of the party which has done so much for the town. He instances the action of the party in regard to the Bolton Council school. Plans were passed and the contract practically accepted, when the Non-Trading Party, thinking that the price to be paid was greater than was needed, petitioned for a special meeting, and at such meeting the original scheme was abandoned, fresh plans were prepared, and as a result about £3,000 was saved to the ratepayers. This is only one of the many achievements of the Non-Traders. When members of the party were first returned the rates—without an Education rate—totalled 7s. 6d. in the £ •, to-day they are 7s. including an Education rate of Is. 6d. in the 2. Without the latter our rates would be down at ss. 6d. in the 2. He is in favour of the widening of Warring- ton Bridge, thus affecting a much needed improvement. He would also support a scheme for the provision of a public Bowling Green in Town Hall Ward, feeling that a green could be made self-supporting and would therefore be no charge upon the rates. He thinks the time has arrived when reductions should be made in the price of gas and electricity used for lighting pur- poses. Anything that would tend to the better housing of the people would have his hearty approval and support; also the remedying of all insanitary areas within the Borough. On the water question nothing can be said until the award of the arbitrator has been received. His only regret is that such award cannot be placed before the electors before this election. Mr. W. Rogers, who comes out as a Conservative for Town Hall Whrd, refers in his address to his " keen interest in the welfare of the town, and dissatisfaction with the present representation of the Ward." He adds that "the Ward contains within its bounds all classes of the community . . . . and demands particular attention from the Council. My attention shall be specially importance to questions which are of such pro- nounced to-day : Economic questions affecting the comfort, health and happiness of the workingman, and questions affecting the education and training of the children. lam emphatically of opinion that each child shall be entitled to the right to be taught the faith of its parents, who alone should decide as to this."

Mr. William Clegg informs the electors of Howley Ward that he has taken a keen interest in the business of the Borough, as they will vote by his attendance at over 350 meetings and he has faithfully discharged the numerous duties of his office. In regard to religion and education he will do his best to continue the present friendly relations. He will continue to press for the demolition of insanitary property, better sanitation, maintenance of the fair wage clause in all contracts, inspection of foods 7 judiciGus publicity of all trading transactions, and immediate action in regard to the Howley and Warrington Bridges. Mr. W. B. Roberts points out that the constitution of the Education Committee is decided by the Town Council, so that it is important to bear in mind the Education Question upon this occasion. He is most emphatically in favour of each parent possessing an inalienable right to decide as to the faith in which his 'children should be trained, and strongly objects to this birthright of Englishmen being taken away. He would, if elected, do all in his power for the Ward. He is in favour of the "Bridges "Question being taken up without further delay, and will support a full and thorough scheme, which will deal with the matter adequately and once for all. He is in full sympathy with the movement for the better housing of the working classes, and considers that this matter has been too long neglected.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

KILLED AT THE LATHE.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at, Earlestown Town Hall, on Monday, relating to that death of Thomas Eaton, forty-seven years, single, of 60 Wharf-road, a well-known sporting rifle shot, who met his death at the London and North-Western waggon works, on Friday, under unusual circumstances. The evidence was to the effect that whilst turning an irregular piece of English oak, intended for the nave of a cart wheel, a piece weighing 231 lbs. flew off and struck Eaton on the jaw; then in its flight knocked Samuel Kynaston, a labourer, walking two yards away, a distance of five feet. Eaton said "Oh," and expired. Kynaston was struck on the right shoulder. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." SCENE IN AN EXPRESS.

When the Liverpool to Manchester express was passing Newton-le-Willows on Saturday night a negro opened a compartment door and walked along the foot- board. Returning to the compartment, he endeavoured to force his only fellow-passenger, a man named Gray, to emulate his performance, and a desperate struggle ensued. Gray pulled the communication cord, and the guard found him in an exhausted condition, with the negro endeavouring to force him out of the open door. The guard locked the negro in the com- partment, but, by pulling the communication cord, he prevented the train from starting.— On Monday the man Wright was brought up before the Eccles magistrates. According to the testimony of Yates, the guard of the express, when the train was pulled up he went to the compartment where the door was open, and found the prisoner doing his best to throw Gray on to the line. Prisoner was shouting, "I'm a nigger from America, and I'll kill every white man." Ultimately three passengers and the guard had to put the prisoner on the floor and kneel on him till they got to Eccles. The prisoner was biting and scratching like a madman.—Superintendent Keys stated the prisoner had left a ship at Liverpool, and bad £7 6s. due to him. The captain was willing to remit any fine.—A fine was imposed of 40s. and costs for interference with the comfort of the passengers, and ss. and costs for being drunk. Gray was allowed 10s. expenses, in addition to his railway fare from Liverpool.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
DAMAGES AGAINST A MOTORIST. ENGINE STARTED WHILE REPAIRER WAS UNDER THE CAR.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, Harry Maudsley, cycle and motor car repairer, Earlestown, sued Richard Read, Morecambe, for £5O damages for personal injuries alleged to have been caused by the defendant on August 12th.—Mr. E. A. White appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Hodgson, barrister (instructed by Mr. Read, of Blackpool), defended.—Mr. White said that on the date in question plaintiff was repairing the brake of defendant's motor car and was lying down underneath the car. Defendant came upon the scene and said" How long will you be." Plaintiff replied "About five minutes." Defendant then went to the front of the car and turned the handle which started the engine. As soon as the engine st- irted the propeller shaft commenced to turn round. It so happened that the shaft was coupled up with an old nail which had evidently been placed there temporarily instead of a rivet and this nail caught plaintiff by the shoulder, dragged him up, and jammed him against the bottom of the car. Plaintiff called out and the engine stopped. Dr. Gray,on whose premises the car was being repaired, came out of the house and plaintiff having been extricated he was taken into the surgery and his injuries were attended to. Dr. Gray said to the defendant that he had done a fool's trick and defendant replied that" it was a good job it was not a manslaughter case." That, Mr. White sub- mitted, was a very correct statement of the position of affairs. For five weeks plaintiff was unable to follow his occupation.— Plaintiff was called and in cross- examination denied that his father said the car was quite ready, and that - warning was given before the engine was started—Thomas Maudsley, father of the plaintiff and John Maudsley,brother,corroborated Evidence as to plaintiff's injuries was given by Dr 1 Gray and Dr. Latham. The latter said plaintiff would , be unable to follow his occupation for five or six weeks , after the accident.—Dr. Walker also gave his opinions formed on an examination of the plaintiff's shoulder 1 that day.—For the defence Richard Read,the defendant. stated that when he asked plaintiff's father if the car was ready he replied " It's quite right now." He (defendant) said " Well, I'll start the engine and see how it runs" and turned theswitch on.—A Miss Jackson, who accompanied him, turned the starting handle at witness's request. They had no idea that plaintiff was under the car. When the engine had been running about half a minute they heard a scream. If plaintiff had exercised care he would not have been touched. There was no truth in the suggestion that witness said it might have been a manslaughter case. No complaint I was made to witness about starting the engine and no' repairs were done after the accident.—Dorothy Jackson, Morecambe, corroborated.—Mr. Hodgson contended that plaintiff's story was exceedingly improbable. It was impossible to conceive anyone in his right, sober senses would start the engine knowing that a man was underneath. Plaintiff had ample opportunity of getting lout of the way. Even if defendant was considered negligent, there was contributory negligence on plaintiff's part. The affair was a pure accident.—His Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for £26 lie. and costs.

SOCIETY.

(IAKES.

LORD NEWTON AND CLUBS.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
Grant to the President.

The quarterly meeting of the members of this Societ was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street on Monday evening, Mr. T. Critchley in the chair. There were also on the platform Messrs. Thomas Wadsworth, Benjamin Hall, F. Timperley,Benjamin Farrar,W.Bibby, C. H. Hibbert E. Malone, N. Cook, W. Haigh G. S. Rushton, D. Donnellon,and S. Bowers,(committee) : W. Atkinson (manager), and J. Jarman, (secretary). THE QUARTER'S SALES. The committee submitted for the members' approval their 187th quarterly report and balance sheet which stated that sales for the thirteen weeks ending September 3rd, were £41,688 ss. 11d, which showed an increase over the corresponding period of last year of £4,707 is. 9d. The whole of the society's accounts had been brought up to the date of the Quarter ending, September 3rd, and, as far as could be ascertained, all liabilities and assets included. They would like to congratulate their members on the result which would again allow of a dividend of 35., with a small amount to the Insurance Fund and Reserve and Building Funds (to help to meet the expense of needful alterations), and a balance forward to next quarter. Depreciation of buildings and fixtures had been allowed according to the Society's Rules, and they suggested that a special committee be appointed to revise the rules throughout and bring them as far as possible in line with what has been found the best in the Society's interests. The CHAIRMAN said the report was a very satisfac- tory one. The increase in the sales showed that the Society was in a state of prosperity. They could :-:on- Fratulate themselves on the success they had attained in securing such men as Mr. Atkinson as manager and ' Mr. Jarman as secretary. They were two good experi- enced men. (Applause.) The SECRETARY read the minutes of the last com- mittee meeting which stated that they had decided to use the room in Winwick-street over the drapery department as a store room. The CHAIRMAN proposed the adoption of the report and balance sheet.

Mr. RIGBY said that £195 Bs. 21. had been expended on repairs and renewals. He would like to know what renewals meant. The SECRETARY said that when a thing got unfit for use they replaced it. The term" repairs and renewals" was used whether they had been renewed or whether they had been repaired instead of being renewed. The balance report and sheet were adopted. The following committee were appointed to revise the rules of the Society :—Messrs. T. Wadsworth, E. England, T. Rigby, F. Timperley, B. Zarrar, and the secretary and manager. At this stne the Chairman retired, and the chair was taken by Mr. T. Wadsworth. GRANT TO THE PRESIDENT.

The SECRETARY read the report of a special com- mittee meeting held on October 2nd to discuss the question of making some acknowledgment to the Presi- dent. (Mr. T. Critchley) for the services which he had so ably rendered during the illness of Mr. Rathbone, and since that gentleman's resignation until a new manager had been appointed. The Committee resolved that the President be paid 20 guineas. It was moved and seconded that the commiteee's resolution be adopted. Mr. LFAC/i said he did not agree with the com- mittee's resolution. Their president received five pounds as a committee man and £lO for stock- taking besides his travelling expenses. It was a deliberate waste of the Society's money. (Applause.)

In reply to questions the CHAIRMAN said 'tile com- mittee were not quite unanimous. He would, however, point out that Mr. Critchley had practically been manager of the society for three or four months. Mr. Critchley personally did not want this grant to be made.

Mr. 'lRow remarked that Mr. Birchall served for 40 years and hardly got "thank you" for it. He prided himself upon being an economist, but as this was an exceptional case he would support the committeo, but he hoped this proposal, would not be made a precedent. Mr. RIGI3Y thought notice ought to have been given to the members of this proposal. He moved that the discussion be postponed for further consideration till the next quarter so that they could come prepared to vote and he would then move that the amount to be given he 25 guineas. Mr. SEED opposed the grant, as it had not been placed on the agenda. There were many members I outside who had no idea that this was coming forward, and they had a right to know. The wages question, in his opinion,ought to be settled before giving gratuities. 'He moved that the question be deferred. Mr. HAYWARD seconded. After further discussion a division was taken with the result that the Committee's proposal to grant the I money was carried.

CRAWFORD'S

EDNA

TIIE LITTLE CAKE WITH THE RASPBERRY CMITRE.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 26, 1907. THE TEMPERANCE QUESTION.

The foundation stone of the Disley and Lyme Con- servative Club was laid at Disley, in the Knutsford Parliamentary division, on Saturday by Lady Newtan, who was accompanied by Lord Newton, Major Alan Sykes (the prospective Unionist candidate for the division), and Colonel Dixon (chairman of the Divisional Asso 3iation).—Lord Newton, acknow- ledging the vote of thanks on behalf of his wife, said she was glad to do anything for the benefit and advantage of Disley, but it was conceivable that there were many !people in Disley who looked upon the acquisition of a new club not as an nnmitigated benefit. (Laughter.) He pointed out that there were members who used a club not for the purpose of studying anti-Socialistic literature or the support of Conservative doctrines any more than the membera of the House of Commons or the House of Lords spent the whole of the;r time considering political questions. Members of a club desired to get such social entertain- ment as could be procured under the circumstances. It was for the people there to take care that the club was properly conducted, and did not fall under suspicion as to drinking and gambling. Such was the uncharitable nature of their opponents that they would not be long in finding out if there were fault to find. (Applause.)

ST. HELENS.

NEW CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH AT ST. HELENS.

The foundation stone of the new Congregational church at Newtown, St. Helens, was laid on Saturday afternoon, by Colonel W. W. Pilkington J.P. The new building will seat seven hundred on the ground floor, and a gallery to seat two hundred will eventually be added. The cost of the building will be .£4,720, towards which £3,384 118. has been raised. Colonel R. Pilkington, C. 8., presided over the ceremony. Colonel W. W. Pilkington laid the foundation stone and spoke of the great progress which had been made in the New- town district. Mrs. W. W. Pilkington re-la :I the stone she had laid previously in the old building. Mrs. Jefferies, wife of the present pastor.also re-laid a stone which had been laid in the old building by Mrs. Ward wife of the late pastor, and an eloquent address was delivered by the Rev. Silvester Horne, of London. Later a public meeting was held in Boundary-road Baths, where the services will be held during the erection of the new church. Mr. A. C. Pilkington pre- sided over a large attendance. and Miss Pilkington received the sum of £5l 19s. 4icl. which had been collected in envelopes. The Rev. Gwilyrn Jones and Councillor A. Rudd also addressed the meeting.

SOCTALIST DEMONSTRATION AT ST. HELENS MR. J. A. SEDDON, M.P., AND TEIE TORY PROGRAMME.

A large audience assembled at the St. Helens Hippo- drome on Sunday afternoon for the annual October meeting held by the local Socialists in anticipation of the November elections.

_ Mr. 3. Gilman, Labour candidate for Hardshaw Ward, presided, and commented on the great change that had taken place in the position and attitude of the people during recent pars. Mr. G. B. Taffolo, an Italian Socialist, moved a resolution acclaiming with; satisfaction the growth of Socialism, and demanding immediate legislation for the benefit of the unemployed, of underfed children, and the provision of old age pensions. He referred to the international character of the Socialistic movement. Socialism, if adopted by England, would benefit the whole world. Mr. Tranter seconded. _ _ _ _ _

_ Miss Margaret Tsf`Millan, of the council of the T.L.P.. supported, and dealt at length with the question of underfed children. Socialism was not the art of division.

Mr. J. A. Seddon,M.P. for the Newton Division, who was received with cries of "Give it 'em Jim," said that the magnificent audience and that enthusiastic cheer was full of recompense for all that had been said locally by the " able-bodied liars" who were opposed to them. (Laughter.) Socialists were that day exercised with a great joy. They at last had secured it that their opponents had come into the open. Hitherto they had been fighting in ambush. Hitherto they had been send- ing the camp followers out to meet them; all the rag-tag and bob-tail of Toryism that bad been hanging ' on to the skirts of capitalism had been sent out to do warfare with the Socialists. At last they were now con- fronted by not only the main army, which, by the way, was chiefly composed of drummer—(laughter)—but they bad now formulated their lines of B,track. They were told by one who occupied a very high position, and who lived by plunder—the Duke of Rutland—that they were Atheists, that they were all thieves, and that they were all immoral. Now that they knew that the attack had been formulated, he was locally exercising himself as to who would be in charge of the various departments. Who locally was to be the defender of religion? Was it to be the councillor whose language in front of the Town Hall had to be represented in the newspapers by dashes? Then who was going to be the local high-priest of the sanctity of the home? (Applause, and Voices: "Go it !") Oh, yes, Tam out for scalps. They have asked for it new, and they will get it. He felt that the Tories made a great mistake. They might out of consideration for their own friends have left the sanctity of home out of their pro- gramme when attacking Socialists, and when they called the Socialists robbers, and dukes and the great people of the land forgot how they and their ancestors had robbed the country of vast tracts of country and treasure. Socialists did want to destroy some homes. The millions of homes which were in slums and cellars they wanted to destroy. They wanted to uplift the people and to do away with misery., No wonder they were opposed. Speaking of the rail- way crisis, Mr. Seddon said that the stupid, silly ass-like conduct of the railway directors was one of the surest methods of bringing about the nationalisation of the railways. They wished the railway men good speed and good luck, and so far as they had power they stood behind them to a man. (Applause) He read the Tory programme as put forward by ',An Rutherford, one of Liverpool's Tory M.P.'s, and said that if that was the Tory programme he would do all that lay in his power to help him to carry it out. But it was ridiculous to say that that was the Tory programme. The fact was that it had been " pinched " from the Socialists. (Applause.) , Mr. J. H. Standring, J.P., also spoke, and the resolu- tion was carried unanimously.

PRESENTATION TO DR. G. W. JOSEPH. -- Long Services as Rechabites, Medical Officer.

An interesting presentation took place on Saturday evening in Friars' Green school to Dr. G. W. Joseph who has resigned the appointment of medical officer to Charity Tent No. 11 of the Independent Order of Rechabites after 26 years' service. The gift took the form of a fountain pen which bore the following inscrip- tion:—" Presented to Dr.G. W. Joseph by the members of Charity Tent No. 11, 19th October 1907." Bro. W. Wright (secretary of the teat) made the presentation and Bro. Warburton occupied the chair. There was a very large attendance. The CHA.IRMAN in his opening remarks referred to the good work which was being done by their adult tent and also by the John Dimond juvenile tent. Fifteen years ago they had only a membership of about twenty-one, but now he was proud to say they had a membership of 210. (Applause.) During an interval the CHA.IRMAN said there was very pleasant duty to be performed that evening. As they were all well aware they were going to present Dr. G. W. Joseph, as a slight token of esteem and regard, with a fountain pen. (Applause.) He was sorry to say that Dr. Joseph was retiring from his position as medical officer of the tent. He had been their medical officer for over twenty-five years which was an exceedingly long time fora gentlemen to be the medical officer of a tent. (Hear, hear.) He would now ask Bro. Wright to make the pre3entation. (Applause.) Bro. WRIGHT in making the presentation said he intended passing one or two remarks on the temperance question and also about the attitude of Nlrs. Philip Snowdon in her speech at the Purr Hall, but time would not permit him. He had great pleasure on behalf of the tent in presenting their worthy doctor with a fountain pen. He referred to the time that Dr. Joseph had been medical officer of that tent and the work which he had done for them. He (Bro. Wright) had been secretary of the tent for twenty years and he had known Dr. Jqieph to be medical officer for a long time before he was elected secretary. (Hear, hear.) When the committee met in order to consider what should b 3 given to their retiring medical o:ficer they wondered what would be the most useful gift for a gentleman of such a business type as Dr. Joseph. But after a little consideration one of the committee hit upon the bright idea that a fountain pen would be a most useful gift as Dr. Joseph no doubt would have a great deal of writing to do. (Hear, hear.) He asked the doctor's acceptance of a fountain pen, as a small memento of the services which he had so ably rendered in the past. (Applause.) Or. JOSEPH said that was a new experience for him, as the fountain pen was the first public present he had ever received. It was a very useful and beautiful present and he would always think of the donors when he wa writing, and he would be able to say that there was somebody in the world who had not forgotten hims (Applause.) it was in 1881 when he was appointed medical office' of the tent, which was 26 yeara ago. (Applause.) He hoped the relations between him and the tent would continue to be as close as they had been in the past. He thanked them most heartily for their kind and useful gift. (Applause.) During the evening songs were rendered by Miss Rachel Peacock and Bros. D. Sutton and W. Barton. Recitations were also given by Sister Elsie Hewitt and Bro. D. Sutton. The duties of accompanists were ably ' discharged by Miss Harriet Hill and Mr. Joseph Rill.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-p/ume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

CHRISTIANITY AND SOCIALISM.

lam beginning to wonder what Mr. Charles E. Parker will charge me with next. First it was mis- representation; now it is deception. Misrepresenta- tion pne can understand; it may be done innocently. Deeet•tion is a hard word; and I am surprised that Mr. Parker should charge me with it. I scarcely think that he would have my readers believe that I am an impostor? And yet, what does he mean? lam hoping that ere long he will be prepared to adopt a more generous attitude towards his opponent, and that he will treat me with the same respect he would have me treat him.

I said misrepresentation may be done quite innocently. Let me explain what I mean. Perhaps the following illustration will suffice. At any rate, it will help to make it clear. Hr. Parker in his last letter says : "The system advocated by John Workman," is that "of giving to each according to his need" without any reference to Ihis faithfulness or energy." Now that is misrepresentation ; because the portion of the sentence I have italicised has emanated from the brain of Mr. Parker and is not part of the scheme advocated. Ido not insinuate.that Mr. Parker wishes to deceive my readers: I simply say that he has I misrepresented me—quite innocently no doubt. If I I were to follow the example of my learned friend I should probably say that " it indicates a carelessness I that is scarcely pardonable in one who takes upon himself" the great responsibility of defending the claim of giving to everyone "the fruit of his labour, tho whole fruit, and nothing but the fruit." I should probably tell him that the hand that penned it was Esau's but the writing was that of Jacob. That, in short, he was a deceiver. I forbear. I have more respect for my opponent. Mr. Parker says that I abound in quotations from other people's writings and fill four-fifths of my last letter with them. And then a little later he refers to one of my quotations as only apartial presentation of the case. When I give the qnotatinns partially, he protests ; when I give them fully, he charges me with filling four-fifths of my letter with them. It is difficult to please him, surely. He objects to my quotations, and yet he asks me to believe his. When I quote from the Bible, he tells me that "it is often said you can prove anything from the Bible," and forthwith proves from the Bible that I am wrong and he is right ! He accuses me of unfairly using a sentence by Maurice to support the Socialism I advocate, and when I give the context he prefers to accept the dictum of somebody else in preference to the writings of Maurice himself.

lam going to give some mire quotations. I prefer to acknowledge the fact that they are quotations because I have no desire to give my readers an impression that I have written it all out of my own head, and that lam ever so much cleverer than I really am. Comprenez-volts? Mr. Charles E. Parker quotes Mr. Sydney Webb to support his argument that Christian Socialism and my Socialism are altogether different, one from the other. He evidently prefers to accept Mr. Webb's interpreta- tion of Christian Socialism rather than that of Mr. Maurice himself. Well and good. Mr. Parker believes Mr. Sydney Webb to be an authority worthy of belief. Now Mr. Parker believes in individual rivalry. Mr. Sydney Webb is opposed to it and so were the Christian Socialists. Mr. Sydney Webb says: "Modern Socialism is, accordingly, not a faith in an artificial Utopia, but a rapidly-spreading con- viction, as yet only partly conscious of itself that social health and consequently human happiness is something apart from and above the separate interests of individuals, requiring to be consciously pursued as an end in itself . . . that the pro- duction and distribution of wealth, like any other , public function, cannot safely be intrusted to the unfettered freedom of individuals, but needs to be organised and controlled for the benefit of the whole community." And again:—

"The whole range of the present competitive individualism manifestly tends, indeed, to the glorification not of honest personal service, but of the pursuit of personal gain—not the production of wealth, but the obtaining of riches. The inevitable outcome is the apotheosis, not of social service, but of successful financial speculation, which is already the special bane of the American civiliz- ation. With it comes inevitably a demoralization of personal character, a coarsening of moral fibre and a hideous lack of taste."

Mr. Maurice said, when writing on Christian Socialism in a Tract inteddei to be a declaration of principle : . _ _ _ _ _ _

'" I said that I agreed with Owen, Fourier, and Louis Blanc, in holding co-operation to be the social principle, and competition the dividing, destructive principle. I agree with them because I discover an order through all society which seems to me to have its natural and necessary expression in united work, and its natural and necessary des- truction in individual. rivalship." . _ . . _ _

Mr. Parker once accused roe of a faulty method of interpretation! How strange it all seems ! And what a lot we have yet to learn!

JOHN WORKMAN. THE CONTROVERSY BETWEEN, MR. PARKER AND JOHN WORKMAN.

Sir,—As one who has taken this paper solely for the sake of reading this controversy perhaps it will not he considered out of place if I pass an opinion about it.

I don't profess to be able to prove either contro- versialist to be right to the satisfaction of the other. Mr. Parker again complains of being misquoted, which complaint would be more reasonable if made by John Workman, at least I think so.

To my mind the stand taken by Mr. Parker is illogical, in complaining of John Workman's many quotations in support of his opinions. I should like to ask if that is not always expected in debate, or of what value would a statement be, if unsupported by some authority? The controversy now seems to be, as to whether the Socialism of Maurice and Kingsley was the same as that of John Workman and I am of opinion that John Workman has proved it to be so •, and because of it Mr. Parker is a little piqued, and doesn't know whether he will continn; the discussion. Each week I have read and discussed these letters with one of the best workers the local Liberals possess, and we are both agreed in our conclusions. (I mention this as I may be charged with political bias.) I would like to ask Mr. Parker what he means by "compulsory state" Socialism. Fancy a State compelling men to be either Socialist, Liberal, or Tory! Also what is the State? I take it the State is the People if self governed, on democratic lines. Then, the people will compel the people to Socialism, according to Mr. Parker. What logic !—Yours truly, STUDENT. THE LABOUR PROGRA.MME.

Sir,—l notice a short reference in your last issue to my remarks at the Junior Conservative Club, and beg to say that I do not recognize Friars' Green [louse as the source of authority and headquarters of the whole Labour Party. When I spoke of a programme I distinctly asked what was the " authorised pro- gramme." If John Workman has one, let him give the names of all the Labour members who have formally and definitely agreed to it with dates. Let us know how far it is representative, or only sectional. I contend that the voice of labour is at present both indistinct and discordant, and such flippancies and " little bits off the top " as those in your last issue will not tend to make people listen more respect- fully. I agree to some extent with John Workman and have read many of his views and ideas with pleasure, but I don't think anything will be gained by Labour acting in a spirit of antagonism to either Conservatives or Liberals. Labour is entitled t) all the representation it can get by fair and constitutional means, but it is not entitled to dominate and direct the policy of any Government until it has the constitu- tional power. John' Workman says, "the Tories and Liberals are tumbling Over themselves in their anxiety to copy from it." I agree there has been rather too much tumbling to Labour as well as to Socialism, and think it would be much more sensible if both parties would formulate and adopt "positive policies" of their own, and leave the Labour party to work out its own salvation. In current politics there is too much negation and indecision. Instead of looking for points of agreement everyone seems to be looking for points of difference. Yours &c.,

W. CHRBIES.

JOYNSON HICKS CLIMBS DOWN.

- - Mr. Joynson-Hicks, in a speech delivered at Sale- fl recently, stated that, under Socialism, no man would ! be at liberty to go where he liked, but would have to. !I depart to the post assigned to him. Even a man's wife would not belong to him, nor his children. (Laughter.) This was no longer a laughing matter; lit was a detestable subject. (A Voice : "It is the I way you are handling it.") He was not in the habit ! of making statements without some kind of justification land one had only to read Mr. Wells's book where it was clearly stated that "wives no less than goods were to be held in common." IThat is one side of the medal. This is the reverse. Mr. Joynson-Hicks speaking at Altrincham a few days later said he had been severely taken to task for some. !of the remarks he made at Sale on Friday last with regard to the aims of the Socialist party. Mrs. Wells, the wife of Mr. H. G. Wells, had written to him about a sentence he had attributed to one of her husband's. books to the effect that in the Socialist State wives, no less than goods, were to be held in common, and that no binding contract was to be necessary between man and woman. Mrs. Wells denied the accuracy of his quotation, and demanded a public apology and the withdrawal of what he had attributed to her husband. Mr. Joynson-Hicks now admitted that be had not himself read the book. The sentence in question he had found in a review of the book that appeared iu the Times "of September 15 last year. Mr. Joynson-Hicks must really be more careful or he might do Socialism a great deal of harm —perhaps t

PRDPERTY OWNERS ASSOCIATION.

Annual Meeting.

MR. W. HICRUIAN AND TOWN'S AFFAIRS- The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Property Owners' Association was held at "roft's Assembly Room, on Thursday evening, Sir. William Hickman in the chair. There was a good attendance. ANNUAL REPORT, The Executive Committee of the Association pre- sented their fifth annual rep lrt which s:sted that the membership bad increased to 230, and that subscriptions had been received for the past year amounting to £33 Ss. _ being an increase on the previous year ; together with special donations of £lO towards legal expe ses for Local Government Board Inquiries, with a k.alance brought forward from last year of £22 6s. 20., making a total of £65 14s. 2Ad. The expenditure amounted to £4B 6a. 4id. leaving a balance in the bank of £l7 Ss. 4d. Deputations attended conferences at Leeds Manchester and Blackpool, the reports of which were duly sent to each member of the Association. During the year two. Local Government Bard Inquiries has been held—one appertaining to the Proposed New Street off Sankey- street, the other for the borrowing of £461,000 for Street Improvements and Electric Lighting, and their Associ- ation was legally represented at both with satisfactory results. In order that their Association should have - perfect freedom of action it had been kept entirely free from political bias or any introduction of party , questions. The CHATRMAN said his services as president had now expired and he would be obligei if they would elect a new one and thereupon took a seat aaloagst the other members. Mr. W. COOPER was asked to take the chair and n doing so he proposed that Mr. Hickman be asked to again become president. Mr. J. Hria. seconded and the motion was carried upanimously. The CHAIRMAN said he told the members last year that he would refuse to hold the position again as he wanted some one else to take it and intraduce some new blood, but as they desired it be would again accept the position. (Applause.) He would congratulate them on their successful year and also upon the increase of membership. (Applause.) THE PRESIDENT'S ADDRESS. The PRESIDENT then gave an address in which he stated that last year they decided to take no action whatever in municipal elections and they did not. They, as property owners, were accused of being the great stumbling block of the town, and also with retarding its progress. Their object was to further the progress of the town as muck as possible. (Hear, bear.) Continuing the speaker said he was very glad to say there had been a reduction in the rates during the past year. The property owners were the persona whom the Council came to when the rates were increased and they were quite willing to pay if the money Was u3ed in a praiser manner. He had to express on behalf of their Association their thankful- ness to the Chief Constable and his able staff for the way in which they had taken care of their property and also for protecting it from wilful destruction. In i referring to the question of slum property the speaker I said he simply abhorred it. It was not their fault that there was slum property because they had done all in their power to abolish it. Yet all the blame was put on the shoulders of the property owners. It was the fault of the householder. Was slum property small Property? He knew of small property to be the cleanest and most beautiful, and yet supported by very small means. (Hear, hear and applause.) Was old property necessarily slum property? ("No !") If there was slum property and they removed the occupant to good clean and respectable property the property that " shimmer " removed to would also become slum property. (Hear, hear and applause.) Mr. Hickman went on to refer to the housing of the work- ing classes, which he said was better than it had been for years. (Hear, bear.) Never had he known there to be ' better houses for the working class than those of to-day. 1 ; (Applause.) The house that the ordinary working man occupied now, was better than that the gentry lived in some years ago. The houses were in a good sanitary con- dition, and had all the latest improvements. Alluding to infantile mortality Mr. Hickman said it was to a , certain extent due to the neglect of parents. If a child had a good mother its future could be looked forward to as a happier and more contented one than the future of the child who dwelt amidst insanitary surroundings and who had parents who shamefully neglected to feed it and bring it up in a proper and Christianlike manner. (Applause.) The women who went about in motor cars with their pet dogs and fancy cats ought to help the poor people in their difficulties. ( tlear,hear and applause.) Alluding to the suggestion that the Council should provide an abattoir, Mr. Hickman said abattoirs were always inspected, together with the meat, and were always in a sanitary condition because of constant supervision. Tie wanted to know whys large slaughter- house was kept in a better sanitary condition than an ordinary pr:vate slaughter house. The majority of meat killed was sent out of town and the butcher was very careful, and in fact had to be as to what kind of meat he disposed of for public food. (Hear, hear.) Warrington had s very high death-rate and diet was largely due to the stench which arose from the unlooked after drains and also to the insanitary condition of some property. He hoped that their property would be carefully looked after and that they would unite and help each other and make the association a success. He concluded by saying that they again intended to take no active part in the coming municipal elections, and thanked them for being so attentive to him whilst he was delivering his address. (Applause-) A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the President for his address on the motion of Mr. W. COOPER and seconded by Mr. BRIDGE, ELECTION OF OFFICERS. Mr. R. Lunt was re-elected treasurer and thanked for the services which he had rendered during the past four years. The following were elected on the Committee for the ensuing year :—Councillors Barton, Skelland, Pemberton, R. Wilding and Messrs. Crompton, Throssell, G. Diggle, J. T.Whittle,J.T. Barton, T. H.A.Pilkington, F. Pendlebury, R. Lunt, jun.,. P. Lewitt, J. ,Carlisle, W. Brown and Messrs. Griffiths and Gittens were re-elected auditors on the motion of the CHAIRMAN • seconded by Mr. A. B. MARSH at the same salary as last year.

All I could do was to sit indoors and suffer. "At a most anxious time for a girl I was wasting away in a decline. Then I caught a severe cold, and the hacking cough seemed to tear my chest to pieces. "Every change in the weather aggravated ray suffer- ings, and daily I grew more feeble. I could not now retain even liquid food, and was literally starving. Work was out of the question, my nerves were so unstrung. I trembled, and the least noise grated in my ears : eventually I lost control of my hands. "When in this deplorable plight no medicine gave me any relief, and life seemed full of miseries. A change of air was recommended, so I went to the sea- side, but bad to return home in despair, and worn out. I was a helpless antemic wreck, when a dear friend said, 'Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have cured many worse cases than yours—why not try them?' I sent for Dr. Williams' Pink Pills at once, and commenced taking them. With the third box I began to feel some appetite for food, and T discovered that from day to day I could eat more and more without the terrible pains. "I persevered, and when in a few weeks I saw traces of colour coming into my cheeks,l was delighted. Then I began to regain lost flesh, and after a steady course of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills I was strong enough to do work in earnest, and my companions greeted me with delight. I am now strong and healthy, full of joy and happiness, and never feel any effects of the many ail- ments I once suffered." - _

Sir Howard Vincent has had a varied career, for, beginning as an ensign in the Royal Welsh Fusiliers in 1868, he became a barrister in 1876. Two years later he was Director of Crimi- nal Investigations of the Metropolitan Police, an office which he herd until 1884. In the next years he entered Parliament as member for Central Sheffield. For several years he was a member of the, London County Council, and served in South Africa during the Boer war.

On occasions King Victor' of Italy can dis- pense with ceremony. Recently, during his visit to the museum at Syracuse, he turned to Senator Mose°, who was doing the honours with Professor Orsi, and invited him to dine in the evening on board the Royal yacht. With profound thanks' the senator excused himself on the plea that his travelling kit did not in- clude so much. as a -dinner jacket. "Never mind," replied the King, with a smile, "come as you are." And the senator went.

An interesting announcement is that the Earl of Albemarle has finished a full-sized bronze statue of a bugler and a drummer boy, illustra- tive of one of Rudyard Kipling's poems. His grandfather, the sixth Earl, who died in 1891 a general, took part in the Battle of Waterloo as an ensign of the 14th Foot, now the West York- shire Regiment. He was only sixteen years old at the time, and had charge of one of the drums. Coming unscathed out of the battle, he lived to the age of ninety-two.

A new story about Mr. John D. Rockefeller, the richest of American multi-millionaires, and often said to be the richest man in the world, is told. He has been in,the habit of taking his midday luncheon at a comparatively cheap re- staurant in his home e.ty of Cleveland, Ohio. The dish that he' regularly affected was roast beef and potatoes, the charge for this cumbina- tion was advanced Ioc. Thereupon Mr. Rocke- feller, who had been accustomed to tip the waiter 10c., gave that functionary only sc. The waiter ventured to protest: "Mr. Rockefeller, if I were worth as much as you are I would not squeeze a nickel so hard!' To which the millionaire replied: "Young man, if you squeezed a nickel as hard as I do, you would not be a waiter."

Prince Ferdinand, the "ruler" of Bulgaria, is in the unique position of being unable to find a wife. High and low has he searched for a Princess to share his little throne, but without success. He has sought the hands of numerous ladies, Austrian archduchesses, Russian grand duchesses, and princesses of the Fatherland, but without avail. Last summer at Marienbad he had the courage to sound King Edward as to any possibility of his suit being favoured by Princess Victoria of England, only to receive from his Majesty the intimation that his daugh- ter did not, at present at all events, intend to marry. His want of success in finding a wife is attributed to some inexplicable and amazing antipathy which he arouses in the fair sex. His first wife died in 1899. '

Dr. Sven Hedin, the great Swedish traveller, has earned at the age of thirty-eight the title of the Stanley of Asiatic travel. During one of his Tibet wanderings he travelled 6,000 miles, of which only 500 were through known country. His caravan suffered intensely owing to the high altitudes to which they climbed. 'The experi-. ence was weird and aw\ful," say S Dr. Sven Hedin. "Even to unbutton ,one's coat meant acute pain and tension to an overwrought heart, which literally was at the point of breaking. My only safety lay in the fact that I never left the saddle for a single moment from morning till we pitched camp in the evening. Had I done so my heart would have given way. The poor animals, too, suffered dreadfully. Out of forty- five horses I lost forty-four,' and thirty out of thirty-nine camels left their bones at these ter- rible altitudes."
