The Examiner, Saturday, October 26, 1907.

WARRINGTON

Cruelty to a Cat. James Leonard, labourer, of Warrington,was charged at Leigh Police Court on Monday, with horrible cruelty to a cat. Whilst drunk in Garley's lodging- house, he picked up a cat and threw it on the roasting fire. The cat screamed horribly, leaped out, and ran away. It was badly burned and rendered lame, and suffered greay pain. Prisoner who expressed his sorrow, said he remembered nothing ab )at it. He was sentenced to 14 days' hard labour. PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASTURS.—Advt. Curio Exhibition. A highly successful curio exhibition was held on Friday and Saturday of last week in the Dining Hall at the works of Messrs. N. Greening and Sons, Ltd. Eighteen tables were covered with interesting articles, including a fine collection of Old English goods, Indian needlework, and a marvellous collection of Chinese articles. There ,was also a collection of butterflies and several cases of very fine specimens of fish caught by several well known Warrington anglers. The attendance on Friday was very good, and on Saturday the exhibition was open from three to eight, during which time hundreds visited the hall. WHITE CHEESE 6d. rer lb., ASHLEY'S, Sankey St.—Advt. T.4BLE, FLOOR AND HANGING LAMPS. New Designs now showing.--J, S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge- street, Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Express in Danger. Some controversy has arisen as to who saved the Manchester to Warrington express which, as reported in our last issue, was stopped at Ham on Monday evening week; thus avoiding a collision with some waggons which blocked the main line. Monday's "Manchester Evening News "contained the following official statement, furnished by the railway authorities: —" During shunting operations at Glazebrook, in connection with, a mineral train, a mistake was made by one of the staff, and in con- sequence two waggons were thrown off the road, blocking the down line on which the express required to run. This occurrence caused the signalman at Glazebrook to refuse to accept the express on the block telegraph, consequently the passenger train was stopped in the ordinary way, and actually came to a stand at the advance starting signal, worked from Irlam station cabin, and was not permitted to reach the spot where the obstruction existed. The driver of the train obeyed the fixed signal, and at the inquiry which has been held stated that although his mate heard a man shouting on the one side, and a signalman got on the step of his engine telling him some waggons were off the road ahead, he replied, All right ; 1 see what is wrong.' There was, therefore, not the least fear of a collision or anything of a serious nature occurring. For FINEST IRISH EGGS try ASHLEY'S. —Ad vt. PUBLIC BIZTEFIT.—UNDER TFIE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Fire at Richmond's Works. Shortly before ten o'clock on Saturday night,Miatthias Foggarty, electro-plater, 34,Sutton-street, an employee of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., was passing the works of the firm in Academy-street, and discovered that a fire had broken out in the lower packing shop. He at once told the watchman, Frank Naylor, and the alarm was raised. In a very short time the ry Borough Fire Brigade was on the scene, under the direction of Superintendent Turner and Deputy Superintendent Turner. They found that bales of straw and quantities of wood were ablaze in the packing shop. The sprinkler installation had been set to work, and the works brigade, under the direction of Chief Officer Cox, had also done all they could to prevent the fire spreading their efforts fortunately were successful. The packing-room adjoins the most inflammable part of the works, namely the stove enamelling/ shop. A good supply of water was obtained and in site of the dense volumes of smoke the firemen got the fire in hand in about an hour. A splendid save was effeCted, and both brigades and ()thew) who assisted were complimented upon their work. The damaged bales of straw were quickly removed into the yard by many of the employees who, as soon as they heard of the outbreak, assembled to render what assistance was in their power. Part of the roof and some of the windows of the packing shop were dis- troyed. The damage, which is not very great, is covered by insurance. DOULTON TOILET WARE and Dinner Sets. See Windows.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street,' Warrington.—Advt. For PURE PORK SAUSAGES try ASFILEY'S.—Advt. P.S A.

There was a good attendance at the meeting of the Wycliffe Society on Sunday. Mr. R. Mills presided, and delivered an address upon "God's love and man's services to God." Mr. Ewan, of Sale, was the speaker, and dealt with "Ambition.". The address was a warning against unrighteous ambitions. Self in all things was mean and unworthy, but an ambition to work for God, with a strong desire to be obedient to His will, was an ambition everyone should aspire to. Mr. D. Owen and the Secretary took part in the meeting. Miss Hipkiss sang "Come unto. me" and "0 lovely flowers." Mr. Payton presided at the organ. The Secretary gave a very hearty welcome to four new members.—The weekly meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street on Sunday afternoon. Mr. Williams who presided, spoke on the advantages of the P.S.A. movement and the,benefits to be derived therefrom. An address was delivered by Mr. A. Jones. Miss Jones and Mr. Proud rendered solos, Mr. Light offered prayer, and the lesson was read by Mr. Warham. The president urged upon all those who had a vote during the coming November elections to vote only for men noted for true nobility and uprightness. For PLAIN & SMOKED BACON try ASHHEY'S.—Advt. LAtliEs' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Adtv. Anglers' Dinner at Warrington. The sixth annual dinner in connection nith the "Perseverance " Angling Society was held on Thurs- day night week at the Cattlemarket Hotel, Market- street. About 50 members and friends sat down to an excellent meal provided by Mr. and Mrs. Bonnell. After a brief ieterval a smoking concert was held, the chair being occupied by Mr. George Rotherham —The Hon. Secretary (Mr. William Bradbury) submitted his annual balance sheet, which showed that the total income for the year amounted to £26 Bs., and the expenditure £25 13s. The sum of £l4 12s. had been paid out in prize-money, in addition to 14 prizes given by tradesmen. in conclusion Mr. Bradbury hoped the members would do their best to create that unity which ought to exist amongst brother anglers. (Applause.)—Mr. Samuel Lodge, who is on the com- mittee of the Warrington Anglers' Association, delivered a short address, in which he said the object of the association was to get possession of as many waters as possible in order to get more privileges for the members. On the proposition of the Chairman the toast of " The Warrington Anglers' Association " was enthusiastically drunk. During the evening,songs were given by Miss Baidewton, Messrs. W. Bradbury, Gorse, W. Owen, Tarrant, Habberley,Boscow, °leave, H. Owen, and George Caldwell, and gramophone selections by Mr. Woods. Mr. Gorse ably played the accompaniments. THE INFIRMINY BAZAAR.

£2,271 HANDED OVER TO THE EXTENSION FOND.

The final meeting of the Infirmary Bazaar Committee was held at the Town Hall on Wednesday afternoon. Mr. W. Peter Rylands presided over a good attendance which included Mrs. Henry Greenall (president of the Ladies' Committee) and many of the Bazaar officials. Letters of apology for non-attendance were received from the Mayor, Mrs. Roger Parr, Mrs. Parker, Mrs. William Long, Mr. Gordon Roberts, Miss Bessie Dutton and Mrs. Blair.

Mr. MCGREGOR (hon. treasurer) submitted the bazaar accounts, which showed that after payment of expenses amounting to £443 8. 7d. there was a cheque for £2,271 15s. id. to be handed over to the extension fund.

The CHAIRMAN expressed the hearty thanks of the Committee to Mr. McGregor for his kindness in acting as treasurer, and their appreciation of the splendid results achieved. It might be interesting to the Committee to know the exact point at which they had arrived in the direction of the object which they had in view. The total subscriptions, including the £2.271 15s. Id. realized by the bazaar, amounted to £14,675— that was to say they had got within £325 of the figure which they set out to raise. He believed there were many other friends in Warrington who were anxious to subscribe—filled, he believed, with the ambition to make the last snbscription,—and this was an opportunity which they must not miss, because if they did not make baste to subscribe they might be too late. (Laughter.) There were a few extras in the way of alterations to the existing buildings which had been found advisable in order to get the full benefit of the extension, and in that the Committee had acted largely under the advice of the medical Board. If the sum of £15,500 were raised, it would not exceed the requirements of the case. Their friends in the Earlestown and Newton district had taken the matter in hand with a certain amount of energy and he was hoping to receive good news from them. He moved that the accounts be received and adopted and that the amount be transferred to the extension fund. Mr. J. H. SMETHURST seconded and the motion was agreed to. Councillor FULTOIsT-SMITH proposed that the special thanks of the committee be given to Mr. Henry GrPenall for his kindness in allowing the bazaar to be held in his erounds. He said Mr. Greenell's generous and spontaneous offer to allow the bazaar to be held at Hill Cliffe gave the scheme a great impetus and only fine weather was needed to have enabled them to increase the fund to £25.000 instead of £15,000. (Laughter and Applause.) Mr. LISTER KAYE seconded,and the vote was heartily accorded.

Colonel BDCKTON moved a vote of thanks to the Chairman of the general committele (Mr. W. P.Rylands), the hon. treasurer (Mr. J. A. Mc Gregor), Mrs. Henry Greenall (president of the Ladies Committee), and Miss Litton (bon. secretary). Alderman BENNETT seconded and said the zeal which Mr. Rylands bad thrown into the movement was only one of the many claims he had to the gratitude of his fellow townspeople. It was, however, a crowning claim, for in none of the work in which he was engaged was he doing better service for Warrington and the suffering Poor than in his work for the Infirmary. Mrs. Greenall's kindness and energy had been ap inspiration to them all and be thought the bazaar was quite a record in the neighbourhood. The resolution was cordially carried and in respond- ing Mr. RYLA.NDS said the feeling of all those who bad been connected with the furthering of the bazaar was that the-object in view and the success attained were ample reward for any efforts they had put forward. On the motion of Mr. MCGREGOR, seconded by Mr. BAXTER a vote of thanks was passed to all the ladies and gentlemen who took part in the fete either as donors or workers.

The Mayor was thanked for allowing the use of the Council Chamber, on the proposition of Dr. Fox seconded by Councillor FULTON SMITH. A similar compliment to the Chairman for presiding concluded the meeting.

The Health Committee on October 22nd received the resignation of Miss A. J. Hoyle as Lady Sanitary Inspector, on her obtaining another appointment. CHAPPED HANDS. ZIISI-BUK, the Scientific SOOTHER and

HEALER.

What irritation and distress come from raw, chapped hands ! If s woman's skin is at all sensitive she suffers more or less in this way all the year round, but during cold weather the con- dition of her hands becomes a positive torture. First the skin loses its plea,ant "feel," then there is pronounced roughness and redness, followed by cracking and bleeding particularly in the region of the knuckles, and the wrist The trouble is beyond the reach of toilet creams and cheap salves, which possess no medicinal power whatever, and are not of the least use in attacking disease. Zam-Buk is the only scientific and medicinal balm, which, in addition to banish- ing chaps, cold-sores, and roughness, is able to impart a new healthiness to the skin itself. Smearing the hands with Zam Buk and letting the rich herbal juices soak through the pores into the tissues—this is your only way to cure tue skin disease„of which chaps and cold-sores are the first signal. There is also danger of grit and dirt getting into the cracks in the skin and setting up serious inflammation.

-In all cases of raw, chapped hands Zam-Buk is of excellent service. The hands should be first cleansed in warm water without soap, dried on a soft towel, and then anointed with Zatn-Buk spread either on the fingers or apiece of clean linen.

A few applications of this kind 1,411 ensure the roughness And cracks healing thoroughly, but it it usually necessary in the case of more sensitive skins for Zani-Buk to be applied liberally at night; then wear an old glove till morning to ensure the absorption of all the healing balm. Then the herbal juices of which Zam-Buk is composed sink through the pores and carry their rare medicinal influences to the very foundation of the trouble. No cheap salve or toilet cream can ever do this; besides these co irse concoctions clog up the skin and make the last state worse than the first.

Zam-Buk is to be also recommended on account of its absolute purity and beautiful refinement,and its power of quickly restoring the skin to a soft and pliable condition. Those who are liable to roughness, redness, and rawness in winter, should use Zam-Bok at the outset of the season, when the skin may be depended upon to retain its natural softness. Miss E. Garrett, 23, Buckland Hill, Maidstone, writes: - " I used to suffer very badly from cold sores on my lips and deep cracks in my hands. These cracks used often to break open and bleed. Through using Zam-Buk my lips and hands are quite well. For any sore now I apply Zam-Buk and it quickly disappears."

_E BROKEI By Annie H. Donnell.

Crash! Mother's new vase fell to the floor, and two little frightened faces locked at each other in surprise and dismay. Marjorie picked up the pieces carefully and sat down on the ottoman with them in ,her lap. Kentie sat down, too, and they pieced the fragments together. "Why, it looks most like new I" exclaimed Marjorie.

"There's a little teenty hole, Marjo," Kentie said, slowly. ' "But it's so little, Kent! Le's put it on the mantelpiece again and see if it shows." , "Yes, let's."

Kentie climbed up on a chair and took the pieces as Marjorie handed them up. He set the jar back in its old place, with the little hole next to the wall. The piecee held together beautifully. "Don't it look good, Marjo?" he said, ex- citedly.

"Yes, splendid! I don't 'spect anybody'd know it was broken."

Kentie got slowly down, and they went across the room and stood looking up at the little jar. It was mama's present from papa on her last birthday, a.n.d they knew how much she thought of it. They had a faint idea, too, that a was worth quite a good deal of money. " We couldn't tell her now, you know, Marjo, 'cause she's gone to a meetin.' at the church. Let's go upstairs 'n' play party." "What'll we do when she gets home, Kentie? Must we tell right off quick? Maybe she wouldn't feel so bad by 'n' by, when she's had it longer." Teatime came, and bedtime. Mama went upstairs and "kissed them to sleep" in the same dear old way, but somehow it troubled them this ,time. She turned out the gas and went away. The clock on Marjorie's bureau ticked very loud indeed, and ticked quite a long time before there came a shrill whisper from Kentie's room.

"You asleep, Marjo?" "No, are you? I've been awake all night. Don't you guess ?it's most mornin'?" And two little figures tin white night-dresses pattered down the stairs. Mama was wxiting at her desk. It was a letter to grandma, telling "all about those blessed babies.", The sitting- :oom door creaked and mama looked up. What in the world!" she said, for there stood the "blessed babies," hand in hand right before her. In a minute it was all told, Kentie's words tumbling over Marjorie's and Marjortle's tumbling over themselves. It was so much Easier to tell when they had once- begun. Mama listened gravely. Then she took them '-?oth up in her ants and had a little, loving talk with them about the dreadfulness of hiding Things from mama.

"Was it most telling a wrong story, mania?" Kentie whispered. _ _

"I'm afraid so, dear," mama said. "It was a good deal worse than breaking the jar."

By-and-by mama sent them up to bed. and how much lighter their hearts felt going back, and how they hugged each other! Marjorie's clock ticked a jolly little good- night as they got into bed. "Good-night, Kentie !" Marjorie called again. "Ain't it nice we went ?"

"Splendid, Marjo! I'm goin' to try to be— to try to—be--" I expect kentie finished that sentence in Slumber Land, for Marjorie heard no more.

SYCAMORE CURIOSITY.

A sycamore growing over the entrance to a village i a very unusual sight. It can be seen at Clymog, however. How the tree came to grow there is uncertain, but it is supposed that the seeds were blown there by the wind from a neighbouring old sycamore tree. There being a quantity of soil and earth on the ledge, the seeds took root and the tree has grown to considerable dimensions, and is now tended as an arboreal curiosity.

itY DEAR

I have been most pleased with the letters received from my members promising to do their best to help me to provide some warm clothing for the poor during the coming winter. Elsie Talbot has written me a nice little note, asking to be enrolled as a member, and then she says she is going to send on something for a poor child. .Wsll, Elsie, by all means do this. You are now a member of our League, and I hope to receive many little stories from you. Welcome also to Frances Hayward. Last week I mentioned that I should be very pleased to receive anecdotes of some of your pets. Don't forget this. Many of you musi have your favourite cat or dog, or other animal. When I was a boy cats were my favourite, and I could tell you many a tale ot them. Now you try and do tht,. same, and let me hear of your favourite animal.

With love,

UNCLE NED.

TEIE WIDOW AND THE LAMP.

On the west coast of Scotland lived a widow. Her house was high above the water. As she was looking throng% her window she thought of the large ships dashing against the rocks. She began to sa've her money, and she bought a lamp and some oil, and when it was dark and stormy she lit it and put it in her window, so that it might warn the sailors. She soon found that the sailors were glad. Soon she had many presents, and by saving her money and buying a lamp and oil, she saved many a sailor and also many a ship.

DORIS BILLINGIIAIt

THE FAIRIES' LESSON.

Tim had been a very disobedient boy, and his mother had told him that he should sit in his chair and not move till she called him. Tim was in a very bad temper, and being very drowsy, leaned back in his chair. All of a sudden he found himself surrounded by tiny little fairies, saying, "This is. he, at last we have caught him," and Tim saw seated in the middle of them a tiny, stern-looking little judge, who said, "Now then, what do you mean by being a naughty boy, and, when in your : tempers, pulling to pieces our fairies' flowers?' and, turning- to the fairies, Tim recognised all the flowers he had destroyed. "What punish- ment shall we give him?" They answered. "shut him up with the dragon fly." But, then, a sensitive little fairy shouted,. "Let us give him one more chance." The judge thought foe a minute. and then said, "What do you say, i little subjects, to give him one more chance?' ; They •answered,

"Just one ,Inure." Tim. then w:lke up in a frig.ht, with his mother callim? him. He ran and said, "Mother; I will nevei . be a naughty boy, or pull the flowers to pieces a!,7ain"; and he kept his promise. His mother f-,ften .wonders who gave him such a good lesson. MARY SHAW.

BABYLAND.

There is a realm called Babyland—not very far

• away, Where dwell a .race of tiny folk whose life is one •Icnr-' play; L. h.nd of broken dollies, and of headless horse- men bold, • Where nobody has work to do, and nobody grows old. place where fairies are quite real, and dolliee

talk and sing; V,,:aere blittsreups are made of gold, and blue- bells truly ring; N'There poodle dogs and woolly lambs with

melancholy looks Are taught ,their letters .and their sums from

tattered picture-books. We all have come from Babyland—that country

deaf and sweet, And some of us would fain turn back our :Ad and weary feet— And play agatin with fairies, and with babiel _ _

made of wood— In Babyland—where mother's queen, and al her subjects good. '

CONTENTMENT.

Some people are never content with their lot, let what will happen. Clouds and darkness are er their heads, alike whether it rain or shine. To them every incident is an accident, and every accident a calamity. t ven when They have their own way they like it no better than your way, and, indeed, consider their most voluntary acts as matters of compulsion. We saw a istriking illustration the other day of Ulf infirmity we speak of in the conduct of a child about three years old. He was crying because his mother had shut the parlour door. "Poor thing," said a neighbour, compasstionately, "you have shut the child out." " It's all the Game to him," said the mother; "he would cry if I called him in and then shut the door. It's a peculiarity of that boy, that if he is left rather suddenly on either side of a door, he considers himself wronged, and rebels accord- tingly." There are older children who take the same view of things.

A very fat lady, on getting into an omnibus, heard a gentleman make the grumbling re- mark, "Omnibuses were not made for elephants." To this she replded, "Sir, omni- buses are like Noah's Ark, intended to carry all sorts of beasts."

A gentleman having got out of all patience with one of his servants, an Irishman, called him in, and, after scolding him, said, "We must part." The servant stood scratching his head for a moment, then said, with a look of much concern, "Sorry am I that we must part. yer honour; but if we must, may I make €;o bold as to ask where your honour's goiing?"

SHEDDING LIGHT EVERYWHERE.

Lovely as fidwers are wherever seen, they never seem so beautifvl as when they are found in desolate and dreary places. When the mountain climber comes upon some dainty flower on a crag, surrounded by unnielting snows, he is affected almost to reverence. Hum- boldt tells of being deeply touched and im- pressed by finding a beautdful flower on the edge of the crater of Vesuvius. In a little hollow in the lava ashes and dust had settled. and when rain had fallen there was a cupfm of rich soil ready. Then a bird or the wind had borne a seed and dropped it in this bit of garden on the crater's lip, and a sweet flower grew there. No wonder the great traveller was so moved by such a glimpse of beauty 'in such a place. As we go through the world we come now and then upon human lives which seem almost utterly dreary and desolate in their condition or in their circumstances. Yet there is scarcely one such life in which we may not, if we will, cause a flower to bloom. If only we will show thoughtful sympathy, or do some gentle kindness, we shall plant a spray of beauty amid the lava and ashes. If you know a liife that is dreary, that seems utterly desolate and alone, do what you can to get a bit of bloom planted in it.

"Do you really believe that. an ass ever spoke to Balaam?" queried a man who prided him- self on his intellect. Coleridge, to whom the question was put, replied: "My friend, I have no doubt whatever that the story is true. I. have been epoken to in the same way myself." The man of the ti4quiring mind retired for meditation.

Professor Berger stated at the Paris Academy of Medicine that he had performed an operation on a man namedtFerdinand Hy, who had been gaining his living by filling the role of a hunian ostrich at fairs. In Hy's stomach were found the following articles :—One fork, bent double, one button hook, three four-inch French nails; 139 tin tacks, needles, and two steel chains.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

WHAT CAUSES SUNSTROKE?

Dr. A. Dunkin calls attention afresh to the fact that sunstroke is due to the chemical, and not to the heat rays, as generally sup. posed. His argument is based on the fact that no one ever gets heat-stroke from ex- posure to a dark source of heat, and when there is an absence of chemical rays. The actinic, i.e., the chemical rays, are the dan- gerous ones. They will pierce through any- thing except a ray of colour interposed as a filter. Hence, to ward oft sunstroke, one should treat the body as a photographer treats his plat?s, and envelop it in red or yellow—that \Val cut off the chemical rays from the body. An Egyptian army officer who had sevral sunstrokes uod yellow in his helmet and coat, and during five years' exposure had no further sunstrok2s.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STANDARD YARD - MEASURE

Ten years to make a yard-measure sounds a little. extravagant, but not when that "yard-measure " is of platinum and iridium, and is the standard yard-measure destined for the Government standard weights and measures strong room. There a new standard yara-measure has been, placed after being in process of construction since 1897. Upon completion it was subjected to a number of tests, from all of which it emerged with honours. The measure was sent for examina- tion to Paris, also "to the Society for the Construction of Instruments of Precision in Geneva, then to the Board of Trade, to be compared with the instrument already there, and finally to the Mint. Every year for ten years it will be further examined. If it varies by a millionth of an inch it will be re- jected, otherwise it will become a standard.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
NO ANIMALS WITHOUT HEAT.

To speak of warm-blooded and cold- blooded animals is now known to be both un- scientific and inexact, and Professor E. L. Trouessart, the French naturalist, points out that the classes recognised by modern physio- logists are " homeotherms ' and " poecilo- therms," or animals of constant and those of variable temperature. No animals are with- out heat, all being appreciably warmer than the usual temperature of the medium in which they live. In flying insects the excess of heat may reach 7deg. to lldeg. F., while marine invertebrates are not more than a. few tenths of a degree warmer than the sur- rourding sea, but in all poecilotherms the bocly temperature rises or falls with that of the air or water. An important difference be- tween the creatures once called warm- blooded and cold-blooded is that the homeo- therms have an automatic . heat-regulating mechanism. Shivering is a convulsive move- ment of the muscles that warm the body.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
RIGHT-HANDEDNESS IN MAN.

The origin of right-handedness in man has long been a subject of debate among anthro- pologists, naturalists, and philosophers. Some hold that ambidexterity is natural to the human species, and the preferential use of the right hand merely a matter of custom. But Dr. D. G. Brinton, who has been in- vegtigating the subject exhaustively, finds that the cause is much more profound, and that right-handedness is one of the attributes which distinguishes men and women from apes. It is due to the fact that the former are the only creatures who are habitually in an upright 'posture. "The anthropoids closest to men,' he he says, "are ambidextrous, displaying no preference for either hand. But the erect posture intrc‘duces a new dis- tribution of force in the economy; it opposes the powerful retardation of gravity to the distribution of the arterial blood above the lever of the heart. The great arteries aris- ing from the aorta carry the blood in an ap- preciably shorter course, and in less time, to the left brain than to the right. Its nutri- tion, therefore, is the more abundant, and its vitality the more active of the two hemi- spheres. Hence the right side of the body, which it 6ontrols, is more ready to respond to a stimulus on account of its higher inner- vation." ,

LUMINOUS ORGANS OF CEPHALOPODA. Perhaps the most interesting part of Dr. Hoyle's address at the 1907 meeting of the British Association is that relating to the luminous organs Of certain , species of cepha- lopoda. Our knowledge of this subject, he points out, is entirely the growth of the last fifteen years. Like many other denizens of the deeper parts of the ocean, many of the cephalopoda exhibit the ,phenomena of phosphorescence. And this may take the form of lights of various colours on different parts of the body. The flrst record of such appears to be that of Verany, made some seventy years ago, and published in 1851. A cuttle-fish was obtained which emitted beautiful

===== 0002601_19071026_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
BLUE AND YELLOW LIGHT

from different parts. of the b—o-d-y74lllProfessor Chun's observations made on a specimen ob- tained during the voyage of the Valdiva, and quoted •by Dr. Hoyle in the course of his addrcss, give an idea of the beauty of these luminous organs: "Among all the marvels of coloration which the animals of the deep sea exhibited to us, nothing can be even dis- tantly compared with the hues of .these organs. One would think that the body was adorned with a diadem of brilliant gems. The middle organs of the eyes shone with ultramarine-blue, the lateral ones with pear'..y sheen. Those towards the front of the. lower surface of the body gave out a ruby. red light, while those behind were snow. white, or pearly, except The medium one, which was sky-blue. It was indeed a glorious spectacle."

MONSTER MAMMALS OF THE PAST.

i Compared with their extinct allies of earlier, periods of the earth's history,, it may be laid down as a general rule that the large animals of the present day are decidedly in- ., lerior in point of size. During the later por- ' tion of the territorial period, for instance, before the incoming of the glacial epoch, when mammals appear to have attained their Maximum .develoßment, there lived elephants alongside of whcch ordinary individuals of the .existing species would have looked almost dwarfs, while the cave bear and the cave hyena attained considerably larger dimen- sions than their living representatives, and some of the sabre-toothed tigers must have been considerably larger than the biggest African lion or Bengal lion. Again, the re- mains of red deer, bison, and 'wild oxen dis- interred from the caverns and other superfi- cial deposits of this country indicate animal.; :ar superior in size to their degenerate (1::- .eendants of the present day. wh.,e some o;7 the extinct pigs • from the Siwalk Hills c) Northern India might be compared in stature to a tapir_rather than to a wild boar. '1.133 same story is told of reptiles, the giant tor- toise of, the Siwalk Hills. in spite of its dimensions having been considerably exag- crated, greatly exceeding in size the largest • living giant tortoises of either the Mas- carene or the Galapagos Islands. Oldham 5 3 19 *Leigh Shawls 0 0 0 *Runcorn 1 5 17 St. Helens 0 0 0 *Warrington 1 6 90 Widnes A Draw at Widnes.

The Widnes people were more satisfied than those from Warrington with the result of the match on Satur- day. Warrington ought to have won, but instead they only tuceceded in making a draw of the game, and the secona half Widnes came very near to winning 0 in right. There was a crowd of about 3,000. The weatut- was fine until just after the interval, when a rain-storm deluged the players and made the ball difficult to hold. Both sides turns out as selected, tile teams being : Warrington : Tilley, back; Cartwright Dickinson, Isherwood, and Kenyon. three-quarter backs ; Lees and Brookes, half backs ; Shugars, Belton, Heath, Board- man, Harmer, and Thomas, forwards. Widnes : n'earthy, back ; Barber, Lambert, Taylor, and Davies, three-quarter backs; G. Aspey, S. Aspey, Argent, Turner, Carr, and Evans, forwards. Referee, Mr. Priestley, Salford. Touch judges, Messrs. Davies (frlam) and Pearson (Leigh). Warrington won the toss and Aspey started the game. For the first twenty minutes the home team did all the attacking, and were only kept out by the fine defensive play of Tilley, who twice beat the Widnes forwards when they had the line at their mercy. The Warrington backs shaped very poorly, and it was not until near the interval when they got a footing in the Widnes quarter. Here the ball was smartly handled by Lees, Brooks, Dickenson, Isherwood and Kenyon, the wing man running over cleverly at the corner. Thomas failed with the goal-kick, which was a difficult one. Half-time: Warrington one try to nil. The second half was a repetition of the first. Widnes again had the best of matters in the loose, but their efforts lacked finish, and Warrington defended well. A rush by the home forwards carried the ball over the Warrington line, and Noon scOred a try which the Warrington men disputed. Davies failed from a some- what easy position. Widnes kept up the attack, but could not add to their score. Near the end Warrington made great efforts and twice reached the home line,only to be penalised each time when they looked likely to score. The score 'remained unaltered at the close. ST. HELENS v. RUNCORN.

Played at bt. Helens before a good attendance. Run- corn won the toss, and took advantage of the gale that was blowing. The home forwards dribbled away from the first scrummage, and Butterworth picked up and kicked strongly into the home half, where the leather fell into the hands of Flanagan, and after some loose play Butterworth tbrew across to Deakin, who ran over at the corner, The goal kick failed. Resuming, the home forwards followed up a free kick, and the leather was scrummaged in front of the Runcorn posts. Toole received smartly from the pack, and cross kicked to Manchester, who scored at the corner. C. Creevey failed to land a- goal. Runcorn got very dangerous. Atkinson misjudged a long kick from Butterworth, and Wa tton getting hold gave to Walker, who ran in under the posts. Unsworth converted. St. Helens asserted themselves, and from some pretty passing between Hillen, J. Creevey, and C. Creevey, the latter scored under the posts. J. rreevey kicked a goal, and at the interval the score was 1 goal and 2 tries (8 points)

Even play characterised the opening of the second half, but eventually Toole kicked across to Manchester, who raced past Butterworth, and after a splendid run' the length of the field scored a fine try, which C. Creevey improved. Runcorn attacked, and in some loose play Manchester vas injured and had to retire. Runcorn again forced 'natters, and from a round of passing Deakin slipped in at the corner. Unsworth made a good attempt to improve. Runcorn-were penalised, and .1. Creevey landed a goal. Result—St. Helens 4 goals and 3 tries (17 points), Runcorn 1 goal and 3 tries (11 points).

The Examiner, Saturday October 26, 1907. WOMAN'S REALM

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

THE DIVINE SARAH'S RETREAT.

Madame Sarah Bernhardt loves tennis. Every day at Belle-Ile, whether wet or fine, she plays from five to seven o'clock with all that ardour and keenness, all that never-failing enthusiasm, which are so strongly characteristic of her, and seem to put light and life into the smallest of everyday details. The evening meal over, says the World. there are games and music or moon- light strolls on the terrace. Sometimes, too, an author arrives, full of hope and laden with manuscripts, and more than one play which has become famous was first read to Madame Sarah in her rocky retreat. PLAID OUTDOOR COSTUME.

In our sketch is shewn a smart walking cos. turns of black-and-white plaid. The skirt is arranged with double box-pleats down the front and two stitched pleats at each side, and is trimmed with a crossway strapping of the plaid. The jacket is semi-fitting, and is outlined with a narrow strapping. the wider strap on the shoulders being finished with a large button covered with green velvet. The jacket .is closed in front with a .narrow waistcoat of white cloth,

fastened with small gilt buttons, the, corner* being turned back over the collar of green vel- vet. The sleeves are the usual coat style, and finished with small velvet cuffs. The hat worn with. the costume is of fine black felt, trimmed with black glace silk and two bright green quills.

LADY HARBERTON ON DRESS REFORM,

Opening an artistic dress exhibition in London the other day, Lady Harberton, who wore a " rational " costume, said there were signs just now of a reform in dress. One wis that within the last year or two a considerable number of ladies had taken to riding astride instead of making use of that instrument of torture both to themselves and their horses known as the side- saddle. She had also been told that in many of the larger girls' schools the more active games such as hockey were now played in gymtiastic costumes, which was much better in evert way, for they did not have wet skirts dragging round their heels. She had noticed that a newspaper recently quoted the opinions of two eminent doctors on the subject of rational dress. One was entirely in favour of reform, giving for his reasons the dangers that long skirts were to health, but he did not mention the more serious danger of fatigue. V. other doctor took quite the opposite view, stating that if women dressed in anything but skirts it would make them take longer steps and walk like men. (Laughter.) Could there be a stronger condem- nation of women's dress than that? If the fatigue of wearing a dress made women shorten their step cauld it be said that it was a good thing? She would suggest that men should try walking upstairs dressed in a skirt and carrying a coal-scuttle not too brightly polished, and then they would be much more sympathetic towards dress reform. She did not believe the extrava- gant, useless, and awkward cut of women's clothes ever reached the stage at which it was at present; but unless young women dressed in. this way they had not a very good chance of get- ting employment, and she thought that tended to keep up these fashions. Lady Harberton con- cluded by saying that she had cybled many thou- sands of miles in such dresses as that she was then wearing, but she bad never felt anything but the most sincere pity for the women who laughed at her ,because of her dress.

Van Houten's and the Cocoa Supply

Sir,—At a meeting of the council of the Liverpool Chamber of Commerce on 21st inst.' Mr. William A. Cadbury, speaking on behalf of Messrs. Cadbury Brothers, Messrs. Fry and Sons and Messrs. Rowntree and Co., reported the efforts they had made since 1903 to improve the conditions of the slave labour under which the San thome cocoa was grown. He also explained that the proportion they bought, as compared with the world's supply, was not very large, and that if they did not buy it other nations" who did not concern themselves with the method of production" would do so and that he had tried to get one of the largest cocoa manufacturers outside of this country to interest them- selves in the matter, but without success. As Mr. Cadbury's remarks are understood to refer to Messrs. Van Houton, I beg on their behalf to state that they are not the firm referred to, that they were not approiched by Mr. Cadbury, and that they do not use San Thome cocoa.—' am., dear Sir, Yours very truly, ALEXANDER LECKIE. Corre.iponding Agent for C. J. Van Houten S; Zoon.

Women Teachers.

Sir,—The paragraphs on this subject in your column entitled "Women's Chat" above the signature of Madge call for a little more than passing comment, and in many cases for serious consideration. Already the girl pupil teacher who is preparing for or anticipating entering college is discounted at the rate of from £5 to £lO per year less than the lad, although she has passed practically the same examination and is performing the same duties. And though it does not appear logic- ally right, yet it is accepted as correct upon the principle that it is going to be the life's work of the man and it may not be so of the woman, therefore it is necessary to give some more encourage- ment to the lad on entering upon the work. So they are Pupil Teachers until they are about 19 or 20. Then they enter College, and are again thrown upon the resources of their parents, just at a time ,when in most other professions they ars raising themselves clear of such obligations. Most of the girls that I hear about will be above 21, when they leave College. Suppose they assert their womanhood as some young men would their manhood, and either decline to go further, or they are tempted to submit to the Yoke of Hymen, who will be called upon to pay the £2O, or £25? Does the husband take over the liability of the debt as well ! Then again how does the Education Board pro- pose to recover? Is not a fine, the infliction upon a criminal for a criminal offence. Surely that cannot be the meaning of it, or is it a debt to be recovered through the County Court, and as I asked before from whom. I really thought the disposition of the times was toward raising the standard of men's life by better wages, security of position, and all those conditions that contributed to happy comfortable marriage, and the withdrawal of married women from the ranks of labour. In the Post Office they give an inducement to their young ladies in the shape of dowry, for them to marry and make room for the younger ones. The Education department evidently wish to make it as difficult as possible for them to marry. It looks like a very harsh and desperate arrange- ment to yours very truly,

PATRICK O'BRIEN.

125, Wilderspool-road, Warrington.

CHILDREN'S SWIMMING GALA.

Interesting Contests.

On Thursday evening under the auspices of the War- rington Teachers' Association the school children of Warrington held their first annual swimming gala at the Corporation Baths in the presence of Mr. Councillor Fulton-Smith (chairman), members of the Town Council, members of the Education Committee and a large and enthusiastic gathering of the public. The gala was a great success and some keen contests were witnessed. The chief event of the evening was the contest for the "Walker Challenge Shield" which was won by Arpley-street with Silver-street as runners up. Miss M. Barlow gave a very clever and pleasing exhibition of life saving and the art of swimming. Great amusement was caused by Messrs. Ashton and Woolstencroft in a comic interlude. The teachers and children are deeply deeply indebted to Mr. Councillor Fulton-Smith who not only so ably officiated as chairman but also presented the medals for the winners and runners up in the contest for the shield, also to the director, Mr. J. Moore Murray who ' fulfilled the duties of starter, and to Professor Barlow who acted as judge. Councillor FULTON-SMITH delivered an appropriate address before distributing the prizes and he was heartily thanked on the proposition of Mr. W. H. STANSFIELD seconded by Mr. G. A. BROWN. The following is the prize list:—One length boys under 12: 1, W. Cooper; 2, S. W. Roberts. One length boys between 12 and 13 : 1, A. Chalmers. One length boys between 13 and 14: 1, A. Molyneaux ; 2, F. L. Gallon. Squadron race boys under 10: Winners, Arpley street : runners-up, Silver-street. Neat dive boys under 11 : 1, C. Taylor. Two lengths boys under 13: 1, S. W. Roberts. Two lengths boys between 13 and 14: 1, R. Atkinson. Plunging boys under 14: 1, H. Lawless. One length (breast stroke) boys under 13 : W. Head. One length boys under 14, who have been taught by Professor Barlow : 1, J. Walsh ; 2, A. Riley. Neat Dive boys under 14, who have been taught by Professor Barlow : 1, H. Wood ; 2, J. Furnival ; 3, J. Holding. Two lengths boys under 14, who have been taught of Professor Barlow : I,G. Cole. Obstacle Race boys under 14: 1, R. Atkinson; 2, F. L. Gallon.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

The Farmers' Charter.

A pablic meeting promoted by the Knutsford Division Liberal Association was held in Stretton schoolroom on Thursday evening. This was one of a series of meet- ings which the Member (Mr. A. J. King) is addressing in all parts of the scattered constituency. There was a good attendance. Mr. J. Darbyshire presiding. He was supported by Mr. A. J. King, 1‘1.P., Mr. Fred Home (farmer candidate for Ludlow Division), Mr. R. Billington, Mr. J. R. Tomlinson, Mr. Rose, Mr. Joseph' Smith (Liberal agent) and others. The Chairman expressed the gratification which it afforded them that,after such a long period of Tory rule, the Knutsford Division was at last represented by a Liberal. (Applause). Mr. A. J. King, M.P., who had a hearty reception,said many of them came to the conclusion for some years before 1906, that their country was not being very well governed and they made up their minds to do all they could to bring about a change of Government,policy and principle. From what he had seen during the last two years he had no cause to regret the little share he had in bringing about -that change. (Applause). He hoped also that the electors of that part of the constituency did not feel dissatisfied with the share they took. To his mind there was no doubt that the present House of Commons was the best that this country had had for many years, the most hard-working, filled with men all of whom he believed were characterised by zeal and earnestness and a desire to push forward legislation which should do something to ameliorate the lot of the majority of the inhabitants of the country, and many of whom were specially qualified by past experi- ence to deal with the various subjects which came up. Mr. King quoted Lord Newton's opinion of the House of Commons which he said he was prepared to accept as a testimonial to its character. Lord Newton wrote : "The present House of Commons is quite different from its predecessors. The Liberal majority instead of consisting of tepid Whigs and half-hearted I Liberals, consists for the most part of

===== 0002601_19071026_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
ARDENT DEMOCRATS

who mean business, who intend to lose no time and who are in deadly earnest." (Applause.) The record of the work of the last two years quite justified that description. No fewer than 116 Bills had passed through both Houses and received the itoyal assent. If they compared that with the record of the last two years •of Conservative government in which they only passed 59 Bills they would agree that the present House ofCommons had lost no time in doing work which they thought would be beneficial to the country. It was quite true that some great measures on which they set their hearts had not been passed—such as the Education Bill,some of their Land Bills, and the Plural Voting Bill. Although much time and thought was given to them in the House of Commons they unfortunately did not become law, because as we were at present constituted we might go to the expense, turmoil and trouble of a General election, we might turn out a Conservative Government and put in a Liberal Government with an enormous majority and still we had Mr. Balfour, the leader of the late Government sitting on the opposition benches with a miserable number of followers, but still possess- ing the power to thwart the useful legislation the Government was anxious to pass and which the country had sent them to promote. Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman had indicated the steps he proposed to take which would ensure at any rate that within the life of a single Parliament any important measure which the Government brought in should become law in spite of the wishes of the Hereditary Chamber. His proposals for bringing that about were

===== 0002601_19071026_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTREMELY MODERATE

but he (Mr. King) thought they would be effective and he hoped he would have the support of all the voters in the Division in doing all he could to back Sir Henry up in bringing about that very desirable change. (Applause.) The Government had done an immense amount of useful work which would have a very benefical effect on the comfort and lives of many of the people. Measures had been passed which directly affected the interests of agricultural industry. So long as the tenant farmers and labourers continued to send Tories to represent them in the House of Commons nothing would be done for them. The people whose business got attended to in the House of Commons were those who knew what they wanted and combined to attain it and who sent men to the House of Commons to see that their wishes were fulfilled. The Tories were so convinced that the farmers and the labourers would always vote for them that they thought they need not consider agricultural interests. There was a very different record to show of the past two years of Liberal Government. Mr. King then enumerated some of the important agricultural measures which had been passed. He pointed out that the Butt3r Act wonld make it absolutely illegal to sell butter containing more than 27 per cent of water and it must not be sold under any name of which the word " butter " formed a part. The so-called milk-blended butter would therefore no longer be brought into com- petition with the good honest butter of the Cheshire farmer. (Applause.) Every department of the Government had had a new vigour and spirit infused into it under the present administration. He believed there had never been a leader of a party in the House of Commons who had in so short a time gained entirely the esteem and affectionate regard of every section of his party as SIR. HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN

had done. (Applause.) He started well in the form- ation of his Ministry. He formed it on somewhat novel principles, selecting each man for the office for which his experience and ability best fitted him. Never in the history of the country had we had a man at the head of the Board of Trade who had given such satis- faction to all parties as Mr. Lloyd George. (Applause.) He had carried through Acts of Parlia- ment which had been waiting to be carried through for years and which no previous Presi- dent of the Board of Trade dare tackle or was capable of passing. Mr. King touched upon the enormous extension of the Workmen's Compensation Act which affected six million people, and the Criminal Appeal Act which gave every criminal the right under certain con- ditions to appeal in case he thought his sentence had been unjustly inflicted. The Justices of the Peace Act laid it down that in future

===== 0002601_19071026_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
APPOINTMENTS OF M A.GISTRATES

no property qualification would be required.ln Cheshire the Act so far had not had much practical result, but he hoped before many years had passed that they would find the Tory county benches leavened with a very fair sprinkling of men of Liberal and Progressive views. With regard to education, he believed no minister ever set himself so earnestly and zealously as Mr. Birrell to try to meet every reasonable objection to the Bill without giving up the main principles upon which the country had decided— that there should be public control and no tests for teachers. In spite of the fact that the measure had passed the House of Commons by an enormous majority it was so mangled that even Mr. Birrell was obliged to declare that they had no wish for such a Bill. With regard to the Trades Disputes Act, the House of Lords after denouncing it as a most iniquitous measure,passed it without a division and the trade union funds were thus secured. If they looked at the administration of the various Government departments they would find that there as well a new spirit had been infused into the methods of carrying on business. Alluding to South African affairs Mr. King expressed satisfaction that the Government had adopted the

===== 0002601_19071026_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD OLD LIBERAL PRINCIPLE

of allowing the people to govern themselves. The result was that the Dutch and the British were working together for the common good ; they had got the whip hand of the small gang of capitalists who previously controlled the country ; the Chinaman was disappearing and he (Mr. King) believed there was a better prospect of real prosperity and happiness for the country than there had been for many years. (Applause.) In the Foreign Office Sir Edward Grey had given general satisfaction, and Mr. John Morley at the India Office was receiving praise from the Press of all parties for his judicious and wise administra- tion of Indian affairs in very difficult times. (Applansel. Mr. King showed how the Govern- ment had improved the financial position of the country, the national debt having been reduced by nearly 30 miilions with a promise of an additional 16 millions this year. The expenditure on the army and navy had been reduced by 9 millions a year, but all authorities were agreed that the navy was never in a stronger position while experts declared that the army was more efficient than it had been for many years. The tariff reformers had seen that the Board of Trade returns were against them and they had had a nasty knock from Australia. They had now raised

===== 0002601_19071026_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BOGEY OF SOCIALISM.

To his mind it was simply an attempt to get back to power, as they did before, on another issue. Once they were in power the people would find that the tariff reform proposals would come up again and we would have a protectionist tariff before we knew where we were. Mr. King con- cluded by saying that the Knutsford Division did an excellent thing in helping to return a Liberal Govern- ment and he hoped they would do a still better thing at the next election by assisting to return another Liberal Ministry. (Applause.) At this stage Mr. Ditchfleld,a member of the audience caused some amusement by attempting to address the meeting, but he was told that he would have an oppor- tunity of asking questions later. Mr. Fred Horne said that in many Divisions which had hitherto been regarded as Tory strongholds, it had done his heart good to see how Liberals who had fought a hard, uphill battle for many years were now rejoicing in the fact that they had come to their own. They had ousted, he believed for ever, the representatives of the Tory squires and landlords who looked on agricultural divisions as their

===== 0002601_19071026_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday October 26, 1907,

pocket boroughs that went with the estate and con- sidered that they inherited them like the family plate. (Laughter.) That day had gone, he believed for ever. The farmer and the agricultural labourer had realized that they had a right to select their representative and they were not going to take the first man who was brought forward by the Conservative Association simply because he happened to be the son of a land- lord or had nothing better to do than to lounge about in the best club in London. They must, however, remember that the price of liberty was eternal vigilance. They won a great tight eighteen months ago but they had got a still bigger fight before them. When the Government came to them, as the Prime Minister was going to do, and asked for their votes so that they would not only get more legislation but put in their place for ever the House of Lords, it would behove

every Liberal voter to stick to his guns and fight shoulder to shoulder. The electors of Knutsford division might be proud of the fact that their Member had already

MADE A VERY CONSIDERABLE REPUTATION for himself in the House of Commons. There were very few men in the House who made such a good maiden speech and the Prime Minister went out of his way to write him a letter of congratulation. (Applause). He had stood up at every division for the interests of his constituents. Mr. Horne then dealt at some length with the Land Tenure Act which, he said, was intended to put tenant farmers on a fair footing, not injuring the landlords but giving the farmers security in their farms and compensation for all improvements they made. If a tenant farmer improved his farm in any way so that he made it worth more rent and the landlord either raised the rent or let the farm to somebody else, the tenant would receive full com- pensation for every improvement. Then if the tenant suffered damage from game which he had not the lawful right to kill he would receive compensation. The

===== 0002601_19071026_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
MOST VITAL PRINCIPLE.

however, was that in future so loag as a tenant farmer farmed his land decently and paid his rect, he should not be disturbed in his holding nor should the rent be raised to such an extent as would make him leave it. No landlord would be able to influence a tenant farmer as to how he should vote or where he should worship. or restrict him from shooting hares and rabbits, as many of them did, and if he dismissed the tenant for any of those reasons he would have to compensate him. 'The farmer wonld also be able to repair farm buildings and roads which were in a bad condition, if the landlord declined to do the work,and would receive compensation. He (111 r. Horse) believed the Act would be a great charter of freedom to English tenant farmers. He was afraid 90 per cent. of them had wanted such an Act. They had waited for it for twenty years and the Government was entitled to their gratitude for having given them an Act which was worth having. (Applause.) Another object of the Government was so to alter the holding of land in this country as to give the labourer's lad a chance of getting his bit of land and working his way up into a substantial position. They believed if they had a series of small farms from the allotment of half an acre up to a small holding of 50 acres, it would give the men who wanted land a chance of working their way to comparative comfort and prosperity. Under the Small Holdings and Allotments Act that would be possible. The Parish Councils would be able to grant allotments up to five acres,and if they could not get the land voluntarily they had power to take it compulsorily. The same applied to small holdings, except that the County Councils had to be applied to. It was said that it was unfair to take land from the landlord if he did not want to give it up. But that was not so. For the past two hundred years the landlords had been busy taking the land from the labourers. They had enclosed over 10 million acres of common land and made it their own. He thought the country had a right to insist on that land being

===== 0002601_19071026_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
RESTORED TO THE PEOPLE.

(Applause.) The Act, however, did not propose to make the landlords fork out what they had taken from the people in the past. It simply said that when men wanted land they should have it and the Government was prepared to give the local authorities power to take it. Mr. Home concluded by warning his hearers against the machinations of the Tariff Reform League which, he said, was the wealthiest, the most unscrupu- lous, and the most corrupt organisation ever known in this country. He showed how it was backed by land- lords who would benefit enormously by increased rent, and by capitalists who, he said, looked with envy on the American millionaires and wished that they could make such big fortunes out of Protection. They wanted to spoil and plunder the people just as the American manufacturer did and behind them was the unscrupulous gang of South African millionaires who got us into the South African war. The electors must keep their weather eve open and watch the operations of the League. The present Government was the best we had ever had and Mr. King was one of the best members of Parliament. He hoped the electors would keep him as their representative for many years. (Applause). THE BIG LOAF.

Mr. Ditclffleld, responding to the Chairman's invita- tion to ask questions, said that at the general election the Liberals told the working men of the constituency that they were to have a big loaf for little money. He would like to know if the present Government had proved their words. Mr. King said he did not think any Government pro- fessed to take control of the weather or of political affairs in various parts of the world. They did not profess to prevent a drought in Argentina, a failing of crops in Canada, a revolution in Russia or the other causes which had contributed to the present scarcity of wheat. The prices of wheat must fluctuate according to the climate and the supply. The Liberals would not allow any Government to put a tax on wheat or food stuffs coming into the country, because this would be increasing the price by artificial means. He quoted figures showing that the price of wheat in London in March 1907 was 6s. 7d, per cwt. In Paris it was 9s. 4d, the increase being accounted for by the duty of 2s. 101 d. In Berlin the price was 9s. Bd., and the duty 2s. 93d., and in Italy the price was 9s. 7d. to 10s. 10d., the duty being 3s. 03d. Owing to our Free Trade system we were able to buy wheat cheaper than any other conntry. On the motion of Mr. Tomlinson (Knntsford) seconded by Mr. Billington, and supported hy Mr. Rose, a vote of thanks was accorded to the speakers.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"The Breed of the Treshams."

The return visit of Mr. Frank Bertram's principal company in this romantic drama has been enthusiastic- ally welcomed by local playgoers. Appreciative audiences have filled the Royal Court Theatre this week and those who have not yet seen the play should not miss to-night's opportunity of doing so. "The Breed of the Treshams " is full of incident and excite- ment, for it reproduces one of the most eventful periods of our nation's history— the Civil War. We find ourselves with the Royalist forces and hear dark murmurings of mutiny and treason which eventuate in some highly dramatic scenes. One great character dominates the story—that of Lieutenant Reresby, "The Rat." The nick name is not ill chosen, for he is a strange, dare-devil sort of fellow who cares for no one and whose past has been such that he himself scoffs at the idea of his "word of honour " being accepted. He confesses his willing- ness to sell his sword and his honour to the highest bidder and is a ready confederate in treasonous designs. Yet in the long run be proves himself capable of rising to the noblest emotions, even to heroism. He endures the most awful tortures and is ready to give up his life to screen an arrant coward of a Colonel, not because he has any love fur the Colonel, but to save the feelings of that officer's sister Margaret for whom he has conceived a tender regard because she him of his own lost Margaret of the days gone by. We have nothing but praise for Mr. Sidney Bland's study of " The Rat." It has been truly said that he lives the part —at any rate he throws his whole heart into it and every word and gesture has a depth of meaning. Mr. Bland is a true artiste and is fully equal to the demands which such an exacting part makes upon him. He is splendid in that powerful scene where he keeps a body of soldiers at bay by feigning drunkenness and then, when their suspicions have been disarmed, turning on them with pistols. It is a brilliant piece of acting which gains cordial recognition. The horrors of the torture scene could not be more realistically ; brought home and here Mr. Bland excels himself. The entire company is an exceedingly capable one. Mr. Benson North as Colonel Hungerford has an unpopular part but he takes it well, and Mr. Alfred Holles is the personification of dignity and righteous indignation as Viscount Dorsington. Mr. George Traill also gains high honours in the role of the kindly-disposed Sir Henry Curwen. Miss Mildred Hodson is a winsome Margaret Hungerford, her portrayal being character- ised by naturalness and fitting restraint, and she is associated with "The Rat "in some touching scenes. Miss Grace Harlington infuses a fine spirit into her representation of the irrepressible Batty.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====

TRAFALGAR DAY.

SCHOOL CHILDREN'S ESSAY COMPETITION.

Monday was the one hundred and second anniversary of the battle of Trafalgar, and honour was once more paid to the memory of our greatest naval hero. In Warrington the day was one of special interest to the school children. In order to inculcate the ideal of duty, which is the dominant lesson of the anniversary, Alderman Bennett last year presented a handsome shield, embellished with cipper from Nelson's flag- ship "Victory," to be held for twelve months by the school whose representative writes the best essay on a set subject in a competition open to all the elementary schools in the to%n. The idea was enthusiastically received and the first competition—for essays on "Lord Nelson" —resulted in St. James'school becoming the first possessors of the shield. This year the subject of the essay was " Two types of national duty ; (a) Colonel McCarthy O'Leary; (b) John Howard "—a very appropriate one as the gallant soldier who fell at Pieters Hill while leading the South Lancashire Regiment and the great prison reformer and philan- thropist were both closely associated with Warrington. The competitors all sat for examination at People's College and wrote the essays under supervision and without assistance. Annie Wright, of Holy Trinity school, who won the second prize twelve months ago, produced such a creditable essay that this year she gained the premier award with 97 points out of a possible 100 and the shield consequently goes to Holy Trinity School. Harold Greenhalgh, of St. James school, won the second prize with 94 points. Both the successful essayists were the recepients of books from Alderman Bennett.

. The presentation of the shield and prizes took place on Monday afternoon. Alderman Bennett visited each of the schools and heartily congratulated the scholars and staff upon their success. He also delivered an interesting address upon the lessons of Trafalgar Day , and urged the children to look to the examples of , Lord Nelson, Colonel O'Leary and John Howard who i each n his sphere nobly did his duty to his country and his fellowmen.

Miss DAVIES (headmistress) accepted the shield on behalf of Holy Trinity School. The books presented to Annie Wright were Life of John Howard," "Puck of Pooks Hill," " Hereward the Wake," "Stories of King Arthur," and "The Warrington Volunteers." Harold Green- halgh received :—" Life of John Howard," "Britain Long Ago," "Puck of Pooks Hill," and The War- rington Volunteers." At each school an interesting musical programme was submitted. There was a separate essay competition on the same subject at the Secondary School. Eleven girls corn. peted and the prize was awarded to Ruby Witts of Form VI. who obtained 84 marks. She was presented with the following books :—" Life of John Howard," "History of the Warrington Volunteers," " Life of Sir George White," "Hereward the Wake" and "Lays of Ancient Rome." The boys were also invited to compete but only two did so and the standard reached was not sufficiently high to justify the awarding of the prizes offered. Harry Davies wrote the better paper and he was presented with a book by the Principal (Mr. E. S. A. Robson).

===== 0002601_19071026_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE OF THE WINNING ESSAYS.

We have pleasure in printing the following essay by Harold Greenhalgh, St. James' School, who was awarded the second prize :

===== 0002601_19071026_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO TYPES OF NATIONAL DUTY. JOHN HOWARD. COLONEL O'LEARY.

These two men were both true patriots. Though they lived at different times they were men whose religion urged them to do service for the land which was their home. The self-imposed duty of the former was to inspect the horrible dungeons of prisons wherein existed the vilest classes of individuals. The duty of the latter was to fight for the welfare of his beloved country. The first was a philanthropist while the other was a true soldier. Before proceeding further with the subject we must know the meaning of " Duty." It means this" What one owes" and "National Duty" means" What one owes to his country." John Howard when a boy of sixteen was left an orphan. His philanthropy is shown in his early years when on finding that his tenants' houses were insanitary he commanded them to be made sanitary at his own expense. On hearing of an earthquake in Portugal he set out for Lisbon thinking he might relieve the misery ' of the sufferers which he knew must be intense. But he never reached Lisbon, the packet in which he sailed being captured by a French privateer. He was taken to Brest at which place he remained al prisoner. He was afterwards taken to Dinan when having languished a few weeks in prison he was sent to England on promising that a French officer in prison in England should be set at liberty. When he reached home he faithfully kept his promise. This shows he was a true Englishman and that his word was his bond. The horrible state of the prisons in which Howard was confined is well described in his book the "State of Prisons." He slept on straw for six nights, his room was never cleaned during the week and the food was unfit to eat. In 1773 this country gentleman was made sheriff of Bedfordshire. This was really the commence- ment of his prison researches. Had he not been made sheriff he would probably never have entered the dark and dirty dungeons which were in various parts of the country. In 1774 he commenced his prison researches. For many years he travelled over thousands of miles exposed to dangers of every description yet he never encountered any personal danger but once, an account of which I will now relate. The ship in which Howard was travelling was returning from Turkey when one of the crew observed a pirate vessel in full pursuit. All on board became alarmed but Howard kept cool. There was only one gun on board and none of the crew I would fire it. But Howard who possessed more courage than most men fired it. The pirate thinking she was well armed turned her bow in another direction. This illustrates Howard's bravery. If he had not been brave he would never have journeyed from prison to prison risking his life among the robbers for the cause of his fellow men. First of all Howard began by examining the prisons in England, where he found that vermin and vice abounded and gaol fever—a most dreaded disease —had carried off more than three hundred persons in one large prison alone in less than forty hours. Strangely enough Haward escaped this terrible disease, and this perhaps was owing to his temperate and cleanly habits. In England he noticed that fees were charged and that debtors were allowed no food, but had to stand at barred windows to ask for alms, and so in this way earn their scanty living. He also noticed a man who had been declared innocent by the judge, languishing in , prison because he was unable to pay Ike fees required of , him.fie next went to Scotland and Ireland but found to his disgust that the prisons were in a similiar state to those' of England. The fee system was practised, the prisons contained no baths, the dungeons were damp and dirty land were often filled with obnoxious smells. When he noticed that fees were charged everywhere in the British Isles he appealed to the Government to have them abolished. This it did, but the order was not strictly obeyed. Having thoroughly inspected the prisons of the British Isles, he began to make a tour through Europe in the year 1776. In this tour he went through France, Spain, Italy and Holland examining prisons and jotting down the good and bad points seen in every 'prison. The prisons of Holland were the best in Europe. The rooms were neat and clean the clothes which the prisoners wore were good and their food was wholesome. The male prisoners in Holland were made to work in rasp houses and the females in spinning houses but the English prisoners were lazy. The Dutch prisoners were in solitary confinement when work was ever but the prisoners in England were kept all together in one room, never having any work to do. Over the door of the Dutch prisons was the following inscription, "Make them diligent and they will be honest." . Holland was Howard's favourite country and he visited it many times during his researches on the Continent. He next visited Russia where he found the prisons in a wretched condition ; they were worse than those of England. He then went to the east of the Continent. Here he visited hospitals and lazarettoes. The sick were very poorly attended to and their clothing was scanty. Their beds were mostly com- posed of straw and their rooms were unhealthy. No air could penetrate into those abodes of misery and no friend ever visited them. Here Howard spent his last days tending to the sick and wounded. The wounded received poor treatment and their wounds were improperly dressed. Not far from one of these hospitals lived a woman who was very ill. Howard had visited her twice and was called out to visit her again. It was raining when he started but be heeded it not ; he would face anything for the love of mankind. When he came home again be found he had caught a chill and three days afterwards passed away. He said that no po-up was to be used at his burial. He was buried at Cherson in Russian Tartary in the year 1790. Nothing better indicates the character of John Howard than his epitaph, " Whoever thou art, thou standest at the tomb of thy friend."

Colonel O'Leary was a man whose heart was in his regiment, his profession and his duty. He possessed unflinching courage which was always shown when a dangerous task was to he accomplished. He respected his regiment and did all in his power to make it the best and as a consequence he was much loved by his subordinates. It was on a dull morning when Colonel O'Leary and his men were ordered to attack Pieter's Hill which was held by very determined Boers who meant to keep it if they could. But they had a famous fighting regiment to face,commanded by a brave colonel

whose undaunted courage urged him on to face death for the cause of his country. The Colonel who had already revived the spirits of his men by his influence, led them nearer to the enemy. While doing this he was shot and his last words were "Remember men, the eyes of Lancashire are upon us." Then he died. His death was much regretted for his men were devoted to him. It was through O'Leary that the regiment gained the title of the "Regiment that never slept," owing to the excellence of their fighting and the hard work they executed when not engaged. General Buller in speaking of the Colonel said "No regiment was ever better led than the South Lancashires were led by Colonel O'Leary." The following touching words quoted at the visit of General Buller when he unveiled the statute of Colonel O'Leary, show clearly the great qualities of him who firmly stuck to duty. He sleeps his last sleep,he has fought his last battle. No sound shall awake him to glory again, The roar of the cannon the niusketrys' rattle, Shall burst on the ear of our hero in vain.

Ah ! Gone is your leader for ever and ever, Remember his greatness, recall his renown ; His courage and skill, his persistent endeavour ; His deeds are his country's, his glory your own. In the foregoing narrative I have endeavoured to show how these gallant men, whose words were their bond and whose love for their country made them offer their lives for its well-doing, did their duty. They have set us a good example and marked out a path which all should try to tread if they love the land of their birth.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By "()vast."

It is over a month since Warrington won a League match. The fact is disquieting. Moreover the team is obviously not displaying the form which characterised the opening matches of the season. That clever com- bination which so demoralised earlier opponents is lacking, and instead there is a good deal of the kick and rush method. Hiw vital combination is the All Blacks are proving in every match. They pass persistently, and their clever handling brings tries and victories in its train. Their example should improve the Northern Union game.

There was a remarkable contrast between the match at Widnes on Wednesday against the All Blacks, and the one on Saturday against Warrington. The mid-week game was full of open play and fascinat- ing movements. Even Widnes played a distinctly classy game. But on Saturday they were not to be recognised as the,, same side, and Warrington were no better. The exhibition of football given by the two teams was enough to make a New Zealander weep. Any amount of energy was displayed but precious little skill. One passing movement, only was witnessed during the afternoon and that brought a try to Warrington. The handling on both sides was very plor. Some of the Warrington men seemed quite unable to hold the ball, Brooks in particular making a bucketful of mistakes.

At half-time, Warrington were a try ahead as the result of just one solit try gleam of the old-time com- bination. They might have won by this narrow margin if even the defence had been as sure as usual. But a shower made matters worse, and in the second half there was a lot of fumbling. One series of mistakes let the home forwards in, and they rushed the ball over, though the scorer did not appear to touch down. However, like Mercutio's wound, it sufficed, and thereafter Widnes beat Warrington in loose play and cams near to victory several times. Warrington did attack twice with some vigour, but penalties were incurred which gave the Widnes men relief. Warrington seem to have the knack of getting penalised on the line this season, and it is by no means a pleasing feature of their football.

r Tilley was the best man on the field, and his display delighted both Widnes and Warrington spectators. With him less reliable and Warrington must have come away with less credit than they did. Cartwright played a .good game at threequarter and made progress whenever possible, but the centres and halves were all weak. No fault can be found with the forwards who worked hard and beat the home pack for possession. Lally played a smart game for Widnes and with a more experienced three- quarter line behind him his work must have brought points. The Widnes team this season is composed of young players in the main, and they seem likely to train on into a useful side.
