Warrington enter upon the second round of the Lancashire Cup to-day, their opponents being Leigh at Leigh. After beating Runcorn away, Warrington ought to be equal to the task of defeating the Mather- lane organization. I fully expect them to win, especi,dly as there is understood to be a tempting bonus for the men.

The team which did so well at Runcorn and so badly at Widnes has been chosen again. It is a good, sound side, but it will want strengthening for encounters against the stronger clubs who will be left in the Lancashire Cup Competition. The team is : Tilley, full back; Kenyon, Isherwood, Dickenson and Cartwright, three-quarters ; Lees and Brooks, half backs ; Thomas, Boardulin, Heath, Harmer, Shugars and Belton, forwards.

It appears likely that the semi-finals will furnish a tremendous struggle between Warrington, Oldham, Salford and Broughton Rangers, though there is an outside chance that Oldham will be the only home team to win.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

This handsome place of entertainment increases in popularity and during the past week large audiences have enjoyed a programme of exceptional attractive- ness. The best feature is a wonderful entertainment by Rapoli who is described as the world's greatest artistic and sensatimal juggler. He performs some feats which at first sight would appear to be impos- sible. He balances a couple of eggs one on top of the other, on the end of a billiard cue, and then balances the thin end of another cue on the topmost egg. As a finishing touch Rapoli gradually raises them until the bottom cue rests on his chin and then he lowets them to the stage and breaks the eggs in view of the audience. Another sensational trick is accomplished with cannon balls. These are thrown into the air by an ass:stant and Rapoli skil- fully catches them on his neck and hurls them forward. A highly amusing turn is Casey's Circus which represents a street urchin's idea of pro- ducing a Vaudeville entertainment. All the stock features of a circus entertainment are caricatured in a ludicrous fashion and the audience is convulsed. Elsie Steadman is a clever commedienne and dancer and Winifred Ward, who impersonates the immaculate boy sang very creditably. Sydney Lee, aptly described as the gay deceiver, mystifies the audience with his conjuring tricks. The Lick and Ford Trio excel as gymnasts and ccntortionists. Miss Lilly Pearl and Co., present " The Red Letter "which depicts a highly sensational epis,-)de in New York Society. It is exceptionally well produced, the characters being taken by the following : —Samson Whyte, Mr. H. Leslie Ball, Harry Shaw, Mr. Frederick Wilson ; Cecil Bartnell, secretary to Mr. Whyte and secretly a member of the Monkeaston gang, Mr. Frank Chapman ; constable, Mr. George Harrison, and Evelyn Sham, Miss Lilly Pearl. There are some most interesting animated pictures and Widdup and his orchestra give capital selections.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

The Rev. Sidney Pitt occupied the pulpit at this church on Sunday and took for his evening subject : "The launch of a liner." He described the launch of the Cunarder as he had repeatedly seen it on the Clyde. He stated that the ship was a picture of life and showed that in the life, as in the ship, the success depended upon structure. cargo, and navigation.—The solo "Come unto Intina " (Coenen) was rendered ia a pleasing manner by Miss Maud Hipkiss. Mr. W. Pearce presided at the organ._ _

===== 0002601_19071026_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Wesleyan Home Missions.

The fifty-second annual report of the Wesleyan Home Mission Department, which will be issued this week, states that the deep and widespread interest taken by the Methodist Church in aggressive home mission work has resulted in a net accession of 57,000 members to the Church in the last decade. Last year the Liverpool district raised for home mission purposes £2,093, towards which the Warrington circuit contributed £4S; Runcorn, £57 ; Frodsham, £l7 ; Widnes, £32 ; St. Helens, £l6O.

===== 0002601_19071026_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe Church.—Harvest Festival.

The harvest thanksgiving service 4 were held at this Church on Sunday, when there were large congrega- tions. The church was prettily decorated with flowers and plants. The Rev. John Yonge was the preacher both morning and evening. At the morning service he took for his tea " o,that men would praise the Lord for His goodness and for His wonderful works to the children of men. And let them sacrifice the sacrifice of thanks- giving and delcare His works with rejoicing." Psalm cvii., 21-22. He dwelt upon that sense of grati- tude to God which the harvest season should serve to awaken and develop. The theme, he said, was one which did not possess the charm of novelty, it was almost impossible for anybody who spoke of it to present a single fresh thought. That, however, should not mean that the theme was either unimportant or uninteresting. Some of life's best things—its most valuable and most highly prized—were amongst its commonest and most familiar. Moreover, did they not some- times need to force themselves to meditate upon them, to turn them over diligently in their own minds lest they should become dull or even stolid and dead to their value and influence? The blessings of daily life were those which nearest affected their well being. For whenever they shouPf fail, their exceeding value would be at once per- ceived. If they remembered, that spirit of forgetful ingratitude—that failure to realize the constant good- ness of Jehovah, was the prevalent temper of the ancient people. Most of the afflictions and disasters which befel them were traceable to their time-long lack of the grateful spirit towards their God. Seasons of prosperity acted upon them as occasions for vain glory. They became puffed up, self satisfied, and self sufficient, even arragaut and defiant, heedless of the righteous requirements of the God of their fathers. Might we not wisely consider whether in this new age there was any reproduction of this old world evil ? Had it any present day similitudes ? Were we reluctant and slow to praise the Lord for His goodness? Were we sparing aud poor in our sacrifices at the altar ? During that week at the great record meetings of the Congregational Union at Blackpool his attention had been drawn to such inquiry as this. Many words of remonstrance were spoken concerning the prosperity of Christian people in these days being often even lavishly expended in personal aggmndisenunt and indul- gence —while the Cause of Christ and humanity was hindered and fattered by deficiency in means. Men like Dr. Horton and Mr. Jowett, occupying positions of high and deserved prominence in their denomination, found it needful to protest strenuously against the lavish way in which many religious people launch out in selfish and son al gratification—while their attitude toward the claims of Jesus Christ, and His oause in the world was cool and indifferent. Men had increases and still were progressing in material means while they made no proportionate advance in spiritual devotion and consecration. If this ancient Psalmist were on earth to-day he would still need to deplore the reluctance of the Lord's own people to " Praise Him for His good- ness." Still the " Sacrifices of thanksgiving " were either almost entirely withheld—or were measured by an economy which was not always manifested in the externals of living, in the daily expenditure, in the pursuit of personal and social pleasure—in the delights of travel in the varied occupations and developments of modern life which involved outlay. The depressing fact met them in every religious journal—that the churches were comparatively few which were financially prosperous and distinctly generJus. The great Christian Societies were under the perpetual necessity of special appeals. Did not that point only too unmistakably to the reluctance of even those who deemed themselves religious people to give the first place in their practical and pecuniary regard to what they themselves believed to be the cause of Jesus Christ. The goodness of God p to them in their personal lives, concluded the preacher, called for more than a thanksgiying in vocal praise or than a rejoicing in floral decoration. His temporal bounties in repetition of the fruits of the earth, in the continuance of their own life and the life of their dear ones—possibly in special interpositions in personal or family life, in the maintenance, still more in any augmentation of their worldly means—God's material blessings should impel their material offerings. At the evening service the choir very creditably sang the anthems " 0, gladsome light" (Sullivan) " Sing to the Lord of harvest "(Barnby) and " Praise the Lord, 0, Jesusalem " (E. V. Hall). In the latter the tenor solo was interpreted by Mr. Nathan Cook and a quartette was sung by Miss Knowles, Miss A. Wallington, Mr.

N. Cook and Mr. B. Hibbert. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. presided at the organ. s The Rev. J. Yonge preached on the wordn "Whatsoever a man soweth that shall he also reaP', He showed that it would not do to leave the making a their "calling and election sure" to the closing soanesf of life. It would not result in an assured harvest (;) eternal life, if they postponed the seed time to their H of pro - closing hours. e feared that vast numbers . fessing religious people were doing that kind of thing. They talked about preparing to die when the tire - came, but they could never know when that time wala corns, and even if they could did they suppose a fevi pious thoughts, a few devout prayers, a few compulsory confessions of sin were seed sufficient to produce eternal harvest of divinest life? Assuredly that Wit infinitely vain and false. He did not say the boana less grace of God could not bring it to pass that ash's: - tion should come at the end of a Godless career, bill' the Great Teacher Himself had taught them tha' the harvest of eternal life came from seed diligentlY sown in the period of this life.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINOTON. MUNICIPAL ELEC VIONS, 1907. TO THE ELECTORS OF HOWL'EY WARD. LADIES ANY", GV.WILEMEN— I have been honoured by the Conservative Ward Committee, and by leading Liberal Unionists and Others in the Ward, to allow myself to be nominated As a Candidate for your representation in the Town Counci!, and I have consented to do so. As you are well aware the constitution of the Educa- tion Committee is decided by the Town Council, so that It is important to heir In mind the Elocation Question upon this occasion. I AM MOST EMPHATICALLY IN FAVOUR OF EACH PARENT POSSESSING AN INALIENABLE RIGHT TO DECIDE AS TO THE FAITH IN WHICH HIS CHILDREN SHOULD BR TRAINED. AND STRONGLY OBJECT TO THIS BIRTHRIGHT OF ENGLISHMEN BRING TAKEN AWAY. I shQuld. if electeel, do all in my power for the Ward. lAM IN FAVOUR OF THE BRIDGES' QUES- TION being taken up without further delay, and shall support a full and thorough scheme, which will deal with the matter adequately and once for all, I am in full sympathy with the movement for the BETTER HOUSING OF THE WORKING CLASSES and consider that this matter has been too long neglected. If you favour me with your confidence I will do my hest to serve your interests, and the interests of the town, to the best of my ability. I am, ladies and gentlemen, Tutor most obedient servant, WILLIAM BRITFAIN ROBERTS. Wibiersoonl House. Warrington, 18th October, 1907.'
